  /* === v28.13: Layout decouple ===
     The live-stage wraps the bracket+arena+controls so the canvas section
     is fully isolated from the broadcast-modules section below it. The
     `contain: layout style paint` declaration tells the browser this
     subtree's layout is independent — any reflow/repaint inside the
     broadcast modules cannot trigger relayout/repaint of the live stage
     (which was the source of the "text is shaking" complaint: text panels
     prepending new lines were causing the GPU compositor to re-promote
     the canvas as part of a shared layer). */
  .live-stage {
    display: flex; flex-direction: column; gap: 12px;
    contain: layout style paint;
  }
  .broadcast-modules {
    display: flex; flex-direction: column; gap: 10px;
    margin-top: 12px;
    padding: 12px;
    background: linear-gradient(180deg, rgba(20,24,32,0.6) 0%, rgba(12,16,22,0.4) 100%);
    border: 1px solid var(--line);
    border-radius: 10px;
    contain: layout style;
  }
  .broadcast-modules::before {
    content: "BROADCAST PANELS";
    display: block;
    font-size: 9px;
    color: var(--mute);
    letter-spacing: 1.5px;
    text-transform: uppercase;
    margin-bottom: 4px;
    padding: 0 2px;

      <article class="history">
        <h3>The Founding (Brewer's Reach Pitch, Boston)</h3>
        <div class="meta">A history · league archives, partial · author unattributed</div>
        <p>Boston had a problem and Boston had a solution. The problem was that the Beavers' engineering guild kept resolving labor disputes by punching each other. The solution was an open-air pitch on the salt-cured grass at the edge of Brewer's Reach, where the punching could be organized, scored, ticketed, and — eventually — broadcast.</p>
        <p>The first match was held in the spring of League Year 1. Two squads of five Beavers, one balloon each, no weapons. By the third round both squads had switched gear with each other twice and a single popped balloon was ricocheting off the dam-bracing.</p>
        <p>Old Bristlebeard the First was on the field that day. He survived the inaugural match and went on to die in the championship final five years later, taking three Beavers with him. His Riot Shield is, at time of writing, still in circulation. It has since been carried by seven knights across six seasons. The League Office considers retiring it but does not, because every time the Office tries to retire it, it gets stolen back into circulation by a Raccoon.</p>
        <p>The lobster, by the way, was always there. The Beavers fed the original spectators between rounds because the only food vendor in walking distance sold lobster rolls and refused to leave. The tradition stuck. Lobster Reach Pitch is the unofficial name for the Brewer's Reach Pitch concession area to this day.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— "The Beaver Years," chapter 1, League Library, Boston</div>
      </article>

      <article class="history">
        <h3>The Collapse and the League's Response</h3>
        <div class="meta">A history · "what every Knight should know," league orientation pamphlet · LY 9 revision</div>
        <p>You have been told about the Collapse. Maybe by your parents. Maybe by a neighbor who lost a city. Some of you grew up on a Collapse story; some of you never asked because you didn't want the answer. The League does not require you to have an opinion on the Collapse to play.</p>
        <p>Here is what the League will tell you, and only this:</p>
        <p>The Collapse was, more than anything else, a quiet. The lights went out across most of the continent on a single night. The grids did not come back the next morning. The trains did not run. The voices on the wireless did not speak. The Old World — the cities full of the prior dominant species who had built everything we walk through, who had named the streets, who had carved the rune-glyphs we see in concrete — was, by the second week, gone. We do not, as a League, take a position on whether they died or whether they went somewhere we cannot follow. The League's official line: <em>they were here, then they were not, and we who remained had work to do</em>.</p>
        <p>The continent we now share was, in the Old World, divided. The prior species had drawn lines across it — "the United States," "Canada," "Mexico" — and erected checkpoints, posted flags, and required documents to be carried at the crossings. The lines do not exist anymore. The flags do not exist anymore. The checkpoints, where they have not been reclaimed by vines, exist as overgrown ruins. The animal communities who lived in each region before the Collapse continue to live there now; they did not, in any case, recognize the lines while the lines were enforced. The Yukon Lynx have always been Yukon Lynx. The Sonora Coyotes have always been Sonora Coyotes. The Quebec Moose were Quebec Moose long before the Old World decided what Quebec was. The Yucatan Quetzals nest in the same trees they nested in before the Old World had a name for the Yucatan. We use the city-names — Phoenix, Boston, Anchorage, Yucatan, Sonora — because the Old World's runes painted them on every sign and we have not bothered to repaint the signs. The names are convenient. The borders are not.</p>
        <p>The work to do, after the Collapse, was civic. Every animal community that survived survived because they reorganized around <em>something they could rally around in the absence of the prior order</em>. Boston rallied around its engineering beavers, who already knew how to build. Tampa rallied around its coastal salvage crews. Reno rallied around its remaining slot machines, which were the only things in town that still worked, and that's how Reno still has neon. Phoenix rallied around the desert foxes who had always known how to live there. Anchorage rallied around its polar bears, who had been tending the ice well before the Collapse made the ice everyone's problem. Pittsburgh rallied around its ravens, who were the first to look at a foundry full of cooling iron and ask <em>what's left</em>. The Yukon rallied around the lynx by not bothering to rally at all — the lynx do not rally, and the rest of the Yukon agreed to leave them be. Quebec rallied around the moose, who simply walked into the city centers and stayed. Sonora rallied around the coyotes after the coyotes informed them, in their own way, that this was the new arrangement. The Yucatan rallied around the quetzals because, by long-standing tradition, the quetzals had been the loudest birds in the Yucatan for as long as anyone could remember and were therefore considered the natural municipal voice.</p>
        <p>The League was founded later, when life had stabilized enough that we had time to be bored and angry again. The Founders argued that organized combat would absorb the energy that was otherwise getting pointed at, for example, the lobster vendor. The argument worked. We are here now. We have rules. Please read the rule book.</p>
        <p>The runes — the writing the prior species left on every concrete wall, on every metal sign, on every street pole — the League makes no special effort to read. Some of us learn them as a hobby. Some scholars at the Reno academy have read enough that they could tell you what an "EXIT" rune means or a "STOP" rune. The Float Foundation does not require this knowledge of any knight. The runes are a courtesy from the dead. We honor them by not destroying them. We do not pretend, however, that we are the audience they were written for.</p>
        <p>The Float Foundation can answer follow-up questions about what your specific city did during the Collapse. The Float Foundation is staffed by interns. Be kind to interns. Some of them are knights' descendants and they have stories.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— League Orientation Pamphlet, Ninth Edition (LY 9 revision — incorporates the Geography Caucus's reclassification of the recruiting boundary as "the former North American continent")</div>
      </article>

      <article class="history">
        <h3>On the Origin of the Balloon Contract</h3>
        <div class="meta">A history · widely disputed · provenance unclear</div>
        <p>The Balloon Contract was not, originally, about balloons.</p>
        <p>The first League matches were just fistfights. Then someone — usually credited as a Beaver named "Splinter" Tooth, though no record of any Splinter Tooth survives — proposed that fights would be safer if combatants signed an agreement that they had three "lives," and after three knockouts they had to leave the pit. This worked, sort of, except nobody could figure out how to count knockouts in real time during a fast scrum.</p>
        <p>Splinter Tooth (allegedly) suggested that each combatant carry three balloons, and that judges pop one balloon per knockout. The popped balloons were both a visible scoreboard and a physical reminder. Everyone agreed.</p>
        <p>Several decades later, a chemist in Boise developed a stable, weight-tunable lifting gas, which she named Float Gas. The League adopted Float Gas immediately. Now the balloons floated. Now knights felt lighter. Now there was a chemical undercurrent that could be regulated, taxed, and possibly a controlled substance.</p>
        <p>The Float Gas refinery in Boise is now the source of all League-spec balloons. Boise Brawling Badgers get the heaviest gas as a courtesy. The Otters' gas is the lightest, which is why the Otters move so well. The Foxes get desert-formula gas, which expands at high altitude. None of this is a metaphor.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— "A People's History of Float," p. 47</div>
      </article>

      <article class="history">
        <h3>The Stadium Wars</h3>
        <div class="meta">A history · also called "The Concession War" by people who were there</div>
        <p>Between League Years 7 and 8, six cities tried to buy each other's stadiums. Reno started it. Reno wanted the Brewer's Reach Pitch, because Reno wanted the Reach Beaver mascot to wear a gold visor on a Reno banner. Boston refused. Reno offered a sum of bits that, if it had been deposited, would have caused the Boston league treasurer to retire that afternoon. The bits never moved.</p>
        <p>Other cities watched. Tampa Sharks, smelling money, offered to buy the Boise Float Gas Foundry. Boise refused on the grounds that the Foundry's slag was "still warm." Fresno offered to buy the Helena Glacier Rink and convert it to a citrus orchard. Helena's response is preserved in League Archives and consists, in its entirety, of the word "no" repeated forty-eight times.</p>
        <p>Commissioner Whiskerstone settled the dispute by sitting on every disputed stadium's title deed for one consecutive month. By the end, no city was willing to negotiate further, because no city wanted Whiskerstone to come back. The matter is now a permanent League matter: <em>stadiums belong to their cities, immutably</em>.</p>
        <p>The unintended consequence is that the League now sometimes hosts games in <em>neutral venues</em>. The Reno Hallowtide Hollow, the League Foundation Hall, the Kansas Dust Court — these are all rotating-host arenas that nobody owns. They were built specifically to be unbuyable. They have made the League much better.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— League Office, Stadium Adjudication Committee, official summary</div>
      </article>

      <article class="history">
        <h3>The Year the Foundry Did Not Open</h3>
        <div class="meta">A history · widely whispered · officially "no comment"</div>
        <p>In League Year 6, the Boise Float Gas Foundry stopped producing for forty-one days. League officials refused to say why. The Boise Brawling Badgers played that season with reserve gas — old, denser, less reliable. They went 2-12. They have never spoken about it.</p>
        <p>Three weeks into the shutdown, an unsigned letter arrived at the League Office. It was written on plain card stock, in pencil, in a neat round hand. It read: "WE KNOW WHAT YOU PUT IN THE GAS. STOP." The Office did not respond. The shutdown ended six days later. The Foundry resumed production. No one, including the Foundry's senior chemist, has acknowledged the letter exists.</p>
        <p>The Boise Brawling Badgers have, since League Year 6, retained an additional staff member whose only job is to taste a small amount of every batch of Float Gas before it ships. This staff member is paid in cured salmon and is not, as far as anyone has confirmed, an actual badger.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— anonymous, Boise local newspaper, archived</div>
      </article>

      <article class="history">
        <h3>The Reno Heist</h3>
        <div class="meta">A history · disputed · Raccoons say it didn't happen</div>
        <p>The most-scavenged item in League history — Bristlebeard's Riot Shield — was, briefly, in Reno. It was not supposed to be in Reno.</p>
        <p>The Shield was lost on the field in Season 6's final, after its seventh carrier (Wikrai Wartooth Jr., Wolves) fell. League protocol says lost gear is collected by League stewards and returned to a neutral vault overnight. In Season 6's case, the gear was collected, the gear was bagged, the gear was loaded into the steward's truck, and the truck arrived at the vault with the bag intact and the Shield missing.</p>
        <p>The Reno Rascal Raccoons opened the next season with a Tank named Mok Mok wearing a riot shield with seven tally marks scratched on its inner rim. Mok Mok claims the marks are "from training." The League has not pressed the issue. The Beavers have. The Beavers are still pressing the issue.</p>
        <p>Mok Mok's shield was, allegedly, stolen back during a road game in Season 7. Allegedly. Then re-stolen. Then re-stolen. The Shield is, at time of writing, somewhere on the continent. It is being looked for. The lookers are tired.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— "Lost &amp; Stolen: A Field Guide," vol. III</div>
      </article>

      <article class="history">
        <h3>On the Hedgehog Tradition</h3>
        <div class="meta">A history · contested by the League Office · widely retold in HED circles</div>
        <p>Helena was the last city to be admitted to the League. Helena was not the last city to <em>fight</em>.</p>
        <p>Hedgehog combat predates HED's League admission by several generations. Backyard ranges, mountain-pass exhibition matches, neighborhood three-on-three brawls — the Hedgehogs were running their own tournament circuit for decades before anyone in the League Office knew it existed. Snipers in particular were a Hedgehog speciality: every Hedgehog kid grew up shooting at painted plate targets at distances most League Snipers would consider unsporting.</p>
        <p>This is why Hester Quillstrike, who joined HED's roster as a rookie in League Year 8, was already a finished sniper when she arrived. She set the 412-pace record in HED's inaugural season at the Glacier Rink. The League Office initially insisted the shot must have been a measurement error. The League Office sent two officials with calipers. The officials confirmed the measurement. The officials apologized. Hester, who had killed three Beavers in the same match that day, accepted the apology silently.</p>
        <p>Three months later Hester died in a coordinated Otter rush at the same Rink. The Hedgehogs played the rest of the season with her quiver pinned to the dugout wall. The 412-pace record was not broken, only matched, by Sundip Hootington Jr. before his own death later that season. The mark is now jointly held — posthumously, by both shooters.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— "Quill &amp; Crossbow: A History of Helena Combat," ch. 7</div>
      </article>

      <article class="history">
        <h3>On the Long Marketing Group</h3>
        <div class="meta">A history · official LMG corporate history · annotated by detractors</div>
        <p>The Long Marketing Group was founded in League Year 4 by a Tampa coastal salvage crew that had recovered, intact, a single working broadcast tower. The tower had survived the Collapse only because it had been buried under a hurricane wreck the previous year and forgotten about. The salvage crew dug it up, repaired it, and discovered they could broadcast.</p>
        <p>What they decided to broadcast was the League. The League at this point was a regional curiosity — Boston, Wichita, Tampa, Boise had matches; nobody outside those cities watched. The salvage crew (now incorporated, now legally the LMG) made a proposal to the Commissioner: exclusive broadcast rights, in exchange for promoting matches to anyone with a working antenna.</p>
        <p>The Commissioner, who was new in office, agreed. The contract she signed in LY 4 is still in force. The LMG has interpreted "exclusive" expansively. They have built additional towers, networked them, and now control which matches air and which don't. Their slogan, "If it's not on LMG, it didn't happen," is corporate self-mythologizing — but it is also, in a real sense, true. Off-air matches are off-record matches.</p>
        <p>The LMG funds half of the Float Foundation. The Float Foundation, in exchange, does not investigate the LMG. The Commissioner has, on at least one occasion, sat on an LMG executive's office chair until they agreed to broadcast a Beavers match. The chair was uncomfortable. The match was broadcast. The Commissioner did not move for fourteen hours.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— LMG Corporate History, year ten · author redacted</div>
      </article>

      <article class="history">
        <h3>On the Coronation Cup</h3>
        <div class="meta">A history · the league's annual crown-bearing exhibition · founded LY 5</div>
        <p>The Coronation Cup is what happens when the League's drawn teams play one match for one crown.</p>
        <p>The crown is real. The crown is brass, wreathed in laurel, and weighs four pounds. It was forged in League Year 5 by a Boise smith named Pell Whittlestone, who delivered it to the Commissioner's office in a velvet bag that turned out, on inspection, to be a repurposed pillowcase. The Commissioner accepted it without comment. Pell was paid in lobster. The pillowcase was returned to its previous owner, a Brewer's Reach concession clerk who has never explained how she got it.</p>
        <p>The rules of the Cup are simple. The crown spawns at the center of the arena. Any knight may pick it up. The wearer scores points for their team for every second they hold the crown. The wearer cannot fire a weapon — they can only run. The wearer may be killed; if killed, the crown drops at their position and another knight may take it. First team to fifty points wins the match and may keep the crown until the next Cup.</p>
        <p>The Cup is hosted at the Reno Open Court annually on Foundation Day. The Reno Rascal Raccoons have won six of eight Cups, which is statistically improbable and morally suspicious; the four times they did <em>not</em> win, the crown was reported "missing for inspection" the next morning and located three weeks later in a Reno pawn shop. The League has, formally, no opinion on this. The Beavers have, informally, several opinions.</p>
        <p>The crown's interior is engraved with the names of every winning team. Six of those engravings are RCC. The other two are listed under spelling variants the League's calligraphy office insists are accidents. The Commissioner has reviewed the engravings personally. The Commissioner does not engrave.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— "On Cups," League Library, exhibition catalog vol. II</div>
      </article>

      <article class="history">
        <h3>On the Pawington Cup</h3>
        <div class="meta">A history · the league's banner-run team event · founded LY 6 by Mira Pawington herself</div>
        <p>The Pawington Cup is named for Mira Pawington, who is the only knight in League history to die holding her opponent's gear two frames before completing the steal. She is also the only knight in League history to have invented a tournament event by accident.</p>
        <p>The accident happened during a LY 6 exhibition match between the Kits and the Otters. Mira had grabbed an Otter Brawler's Riot Shield and was carrying it toward her team's spawn area to display it (the Pawington Tradition's lesser-known second clause: <em>announce the take</em>). Halfway across the field, an Otter Speedster tackled her, and the Shield landed in the Otter's hands. The Otter took it back to <em>their</em> spawn. The crowd, which was small and consisted mostly of relatives of the players, applauded. The applause was confusing. The applause was directed at the Otter.</p>
        <p>The Commissioner, watching from the booth, noted the applause and noted that nobody in the arena had been killed in twelve minutes. She drafted the Pawington Cup format that evening: a flag at center, two team bases at the spawn ends, the carrier cannot fire, the carrier moves at four-fifths speed, the team scores by completing the run home, and play continues until five caps are made. She named it after Mira because Mira was, at the time, alive and did not object.</p>
        <p>Mira died the following season. She was not playing in the Cup. The Cup is still hers.</p>
        <p>The Cup has been hosted by Kansas (KIT) and Boston (BVR) on alternating years. The Beavers have won it twice. The Kits have won it five times. The Sharks have entered it three times and won zero times because the Sharks refuse to slow to four-fifths speed, citing "principle."</p>
        <div class="signoff">— "On Cups," League Library, exhibition catalog vol. II</div>
      </article>

      <article class="history">
        <h3>On Hallowtide Tag</h3>
        <div class="meta">A history · a Hallowtide-week melee chase event · informal until LY 7, official thereafter</div>
        <p>Hallowtide Tag is the league's only event that bans projectile weapons. It is played at the Reno Hallowtide Hollow during Hallowtide week, in addition to the regular bracket. The event is melee-only because, the Wolves say, "the spirits don't like guns." The Wolves are not necessarily speaking literally. The Wolves are not necessarily speaking metaphorically either.</p>
        <p>The format is straightforward. One knight is "It" at the start. The others flee. The "It" knight must close to melee range and land a strike. Struck knights freeze for three seconds and bleed during the freeze; most do not survive a tag. The "It" knight keeps the role for the entire match; the round ends when only one knight remains alive. Death order determines ranking — first dead is twelfth place, last alive is the winner.</p>
        <p>The event was informal from LY 5 through LY 6 — the Wolves played it at the Underdig as a pack-bonding ritual on the Hallowtide moon, and the league looked the other way. After Crash Wartooth's death in LY 6, the Wolves made a formal request to the Commissioner: codify it. The Commissioner asked why. The Wolves, through their spokesperson (a Brawler named Wikrai Wartooth Jr., who would later carry the Riot Shield to its seventh and final death), said: "Because Crash never got to play it." The Commissioner codified it.</p>
        <p>The first official Hallowtide Tag was won by a Wolf named Kor Wartooth, who is no relation to Crash but bears the surname out of solidarity. Kor finished the match with one balloon remaining. The other eleven knights were dead within seventy-eight seconds. A Tampa announcer, watching from the broadcast booth, said on air: "I have never seen anything end this fast in my life." The clip is preserved in the LMG archive. It is the LMG's most-replayed clip.</p>
        <p>The Wolves have won every Hallowtide Tag since. The other teams enter anyway. The Pandas, in LY 8, asked if they could borrow Wikrai for the event. Wikrai declined politely. The Pandas finished eleventh.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— "Hallowtide Customs," Reno Cultural Office, annual report</div>
      </article>

      <article class="history">
        <h3>The Phoenix Heat Clause</h3>
        <div class="meta">A history · Foxes administrative supplement · revised LY 7</div>
        <p>The Foxes' home field is in Phoenix. Phoenix is hot. The League schedules around this poorly. In LY 3, four matches were played at Skyline Pitch in temperatures above 110°F, and a Beavers wing knight named Pinetar collapsed mid-round from heat exhaustion before being eliminated by a Sharks striker who later said he had no idea Pinetar was already on the ground. The League fined the Sharks for "engagement on a non-engaged target" and fined the Foxes for "scheduling a match at a temperature unsafe for the visiting team's species."</p>
        <p>The Heat Clause was negotiated the following off-season. It states that any Phoenix home match between June 1 and September 15 must be played either at sundown (no earlier than 7:30pm local) or under an approved misting system. The misting system was installed at Skyline Pitch the same year. It runs on reclaimed condensation pulled from the stadium's cooling towers, which is why the field always smells faintly of soda.</p>
        <p>The Heat Clause has had unexpected effects. Sundown matches at Phoenix are now considered the prettiest of the season — the desert sky behind the field goes pink and orange, and the misting system catches the light in a way that gives every replay a faint rainbow. The Foxes' broadcast team adjusted its color grading to lean into this. Visiting teams have started requesting Phoenix matches for the aesthetic. The Beavers have not. The Beavers don't like the heat regardless of clause.</p>
        <p>Pinetar, by the way, recovered fully. He retired from active play in LY 5 and now operates a misting concession stand at Brewer's Reach Pitch, which runs year-round and primarily serves lobster rolls.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— "Phoenix Operations Handbook," Foxes Administration, sixth printing</div>
      </article>

      <article class="history">
        <h3>The Reno Heist, Reconsidered</h3>
        <div class="meta">A history · investigative · Beavers Press, LY 8 supplement</div>
        <p>It has been three full seasons since Bristlebeard's Riot Shield disappeared in transit between the LY 6 final and the League's neutral vault. The League's official position remains: the Shield was collected by stewards, bagged, loaded into a truck, and the truck arrived in Reno with the bag intact and the Shield missing. The Reno Cultural Office's official position remains: the Reno Cultural Office has no comment.</p>
        <p>The Beavers' position is that the Raccoons stole it. The Raccoons' position is that the Raccoons did not steal it but they would like it to be known that if the Raccoons HAD stolen it, the Raccoons would have done a much better job and would not have left a perfectly intact bag. The Wolves' position is that the Beavers stole it back from themselves to make a point. The Owls' position is that no one has properly investigated the Wolves.</p>
        <p>What is known: the truck driver was a contractor, not a League employee, and the contractor was based in Reno. The truck made one stop on the route, in a town called Coalbroke, for a bathroom break. The truck driver entered a gas station. The driver bought a soda. The driver returned to the truck. The truck arrived at the vault. These facts are not in dispute.</p>
        <p>What is in dispute: whether the Coalbroke gas station has a security camera, whether that camera was operational, whether the recording was preserved, and whether the recording — IF preserved — would show anything at all. The League hired an investigator in LY 7. The investigator's report is sealed pending review. The review has been pending for fourteen months.</p>
        <p>This reporter has been to Coalbroke. The gas station does have a camera. The camera points at the register. The bathroom is around the side of the building, where the truck would have parked. The camera does not point at the truck. This reporter asked the gas station's owner if anyone has ever asked about the camera before. The owner said: "Three reporters. One detective. Two raccoons." The owner declined to clarify whether the raccoons were Reno knights or just raccoons.</p>
        <p>Hester now carries a different Riot Shield. The original is presumed lost, but presumption is not proof. The Beavers continue to play. The Reno Cultural Office continues to have no comment. Wikrai Wartooth Jr., who was the Shield's last carrier before Hester, has reportedly stopped attending Wolves social functions. He has been seen at Coalbroke twice this season.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— "Three Years On: A Beavers Press Investigation," LY 8</div>
      </article>

      <article class="history">
        <h3>The Reno Cultural Office Files: What Reno Owes to a Slot Machine</h3>
        <div class="meta">A history · Cultural Office working paper, leaked · author redacted</div>
        <p>This document was not meant to be public. It was filed under the project name "Neon Inheritance" and circulated to three Cultural Office staff and one Founders' archive intern. The intern photocopied it.</p>
        <p>The official League history says Reno survived the Collapse by rallying around its remaining slot machines, "which were the only things in town that still worked." This is true and is also the kind of polished sentence that Cultural Offices put in League Orientation Pamphlets. The unofficial story, kept in the working files, has more texture.</p>
        <p>Reno's grid did not fail all at once the way Boston's did. It failed in slow patches over six weeks, and the slot machines on the lower Strip were on a separate dedicated power line — a redundancy that the casino consortium had paid to install in the late old-world era specifically so a power outage could not interrupt a paying spin. When the rest of the Strip went dark, that one line stayed lit. Three blocks. Maybe twelve buildings. The blocks where the Raccoons had already been congregating because the lights were warm and the ambient noise was loud enough to drown out anything happening outside.</p>
        <p>Within a week, those blocks were the most populated three-block stretch in Reno. Within a month, food trade had organized along them. Within a year, the slot-machine bays were being used as gathering halls because they were already wired for sound and there was nowhere else with working speakers. Within five years, the first Reno-based engineering guild was running matches on the floor of the old Sundip Crown casino, betting bottlecaps, while a working slot machine spun in the lobby for ambiance. The Raccoons, who had moved in early, owned the leases. They still do.</p>
        <p>The Cultural Office paper concludes with a sentence that did not make it into the orientation pamphlet: "Reno's neon is not a tradition. It is the last unbroken light from the old continent, kept lit by people who learned, during the dark, that the cost of the bulb is less than the cost of the dark." The intern who photocopied this paper is now a junior columnist at The Wing Standard. The Cultural Office has not commented on the leak.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Cultural Office working paper, Project Neon Inheritance · classification: internal</div>
      </article>

      <article class="history">
        <h3>The Pawington Arc — How the Cup Got Its Name</h3>
        <div class="meta">A history · Pawington Cup official program, LY 7 inaugural edition · revised LY 8</div>
        <p>The Pawington Cup is the League's flag-capture tournament, held mid-season since League Year 6. It is named for an Otter named Pawington who never won a single match.</p>
        <p>Pawington was a junior knight on the Olympia roster in League Years 2 and 3. They had the position of Striker, which at the time was less codified than it is now — Strikers were essentially the role no one else wanted. Pawington was lightly armored, lightly armed, and small. They were also, by every contemporaneous account, the fastest knight in the League's first three seasons.</p>
        <p>The Otters did not have a flag-capture event in Pawington's era. The League had not yet formalized the mode. But the Otters had a tradition called The Long Snatch, which they ran during exhibition matches: one Otter would carry a colored kerchief from one corner of the field to the other while the rest of the team escorted them. Pawington ran the kerchief in nine consecutive exhibitions and never dropped it. They lost every one of their actual scored matches.</p>
        <p>When the League formalized capture-the-flag in League Year 6 and asked the Otters what to call it, the Olympia delegation said: "Pawington." The League Office said: "We need it to be a name people will recognize." The delegation said: "It's Pawington's." The League Office, after some hours of meeting, agreed to compromise on Pawington Cup, on the condition that no one had to explain who Pawington was unless they were asked.</p>
        <p>Pawington died in League Year 5, before the Cup was instituted. They were not killed in a match — they were simply old. Their kerchief, which had been stitched from a strip of riverbank cloth, was buried with them. There is a replica on display in the Olympia Naturalist Hall. The original is, as far as anyone knows, still in the river.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Pawington Cup official program, LY 7 inaugural edition</div>
      </article>

      <article class="history">
        <h3>On the Widening of the Field</h3>
        <div class="meta">A history · Rules Committee bulletin · LY 8 mid-season revision</div>
        <p>This bulletin announces a change to the field of play, effective the second half of League Year 8. The standard match field is now wider by approximately one-third in both dimensions. The change applies league-wide, retroactive to no prior matches. The change was not anticipated by the schedule. It was made in haste and is being explained in haste.</p>
        <p>The reason for the change is that several knights, in several recent matches, were able to fire effective shots from their starting positions at the moment of round commencement. This was not a tactical innovation. This was a recurring oversight in the field-design specification. The original field width was set in League Year 1 with the assumption that ranged weapons of the era had effective ranges shorter than the spawn-to-spawn distance. The development of the Railgun in League Year 5 — and the redistribution of Railguns to nearly every Sniper position in the seasons since — quietly invalidated that assumption. The Rules Committee has known this for some time. The Rules Committee has now done something about it.</p>
        <p>The new field is one thousand two hundred eighty units wide and seven hundred twenty units tall. The spawn lines are positioned five hundred forty units from the field's center, putting them one thousand eighty units apart. This is two hundred and twenty units beyond the maximum effective range of any currently-fielded ranged weapon. Knights can no longer fire from their spawn line at the opposing spawn line. They must close some ground first. The Rules Committee believes this restores a tactical phase to the opening of each round that had been eroding for several seasons.</p>
        <p>Coaches have not been universally pleased. The Wolves' coaching staff, in particular, observed that their tactical playbook was built around early ranged dominance and that the new dimensions would require them to retrain their Snipers to reposition before firing. The Rules Committee responded that this was, in fact, the point.</p>
        <p>The Otters, predictably, are delighted. A wider field gives Strikers more room to skirmish. Speedsters more room to flank. Tactical variety more room to emerge. The Otters have not stopped saying so since the bulletin was distributed.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— League Rules Committee bulletin, LY 8 R12</div>
      </article>

      <article class="history">
        <h3>On the Camera</h3>
        <div class="meta">A history · LMG technical bulletin no. 47 · production team archives, LY 8</div>
        <p>The League's broadcast camera, for its first six seasons, was a stationary mount. It sat on the south wall of the Brewer's Reach Pitch, framed the field at sixty degrees of elevation, and did not move. The framing was wide enough to capture all five knights on each side, the two goal-zones, the lobster vendor's booth (when she was visible), and the pillars at the field's corners. The framing was, by every viewer survey conducted between LY 1 and LY 6, "fine."</p>
        <p>"Fine" is not the word the LMG broadcast team wanted. Fine meant that viewers had stopped commenting on the camera, which meant they had also stopped commenting on what the camera was showing. By LY 5, the LMG had measured a six-percent decline in the average viewer's per-match attention duration. By LY 6, the decline was eleven percent. The LMG production director — Jossi Treadwell, an Owl, the league's only career broadcast specialist who had never been a knight — proposed, in a memo widely circulated and widely ignored, that the camera should follow the action.</p>
        <p>The first dynamic-camera prototype was tested at the LY 6 Pawington Cup. The camera was a salvaged dolly rig from the pre-Collapse Boston public access studio, mounted on a track along the south wall of Hardwood Hall, operated manually by Treadwell from a booth above the seating. The camera could pan and zoom under Treadwell's hands. The results, by every viewer survey, were unambiguous: average per-match attention duration improved by twenty-two percent at the first match. The Otters complained that the camera "favored the Kits." (Treadwell had, in fact, panned to follow Reka Pawington's flag-running arc more than any other single knight.) The Kits did not comment.</p>
        <p>By LY 7, the Hardwood Hall rig had been replicated at three other stadiums. By LY 8 mid-season, all twelve stadiums had dynamic camera mounts. The rigs are operated by camera operators trained at the LMG's small Reno academy, of which Treadwell remains the head instructor. Treadwell still operates the camera personally for Cup finals. The other camera operators — there are now nine of them, plus three apprentices — are paid in bits and lobster.</p>
        <p>The technical bulletin's note on framing philosophy: <em>"The camera should be where the audience would stand if they were a knight watching from the back rank. Far enough to see the field. Close enough to feel the action."</em> The bulletin is signed by Treadwell. The bulletin has been quoted, paraphrased, and disagreed with in nearly every LMG technical conversation since. The framing philosophy has not changed.</p>
        <p>For the LY 8 field-widening, Treadwell was the first member of the LMG to publicly endorse it. Her stated reason was practical: <em>"The wider field gives the camera more reason to move. A camera that moves is a camera that is doing its job."</em> The camera operators reportedly cheered.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— LMG Technical Bulletin no. 47, "Field Coverage and the Dynamic Mount," LY 8 · Treadwell, supervising</div>
      </article>

      <article class="history">
        <h3>On the Formations Question</h3>
        <div class="meta">A history · Coaches' Caucus · LY 8 mid-season summary, compiled by the Office of the Commissioner</div>
        <p>For the first seven seasons of the League, every match started the same way. Both teams lined up on their spawn marks in a single horizontal rank, balloons aloft, weapons drawn, and waited for the opening bell. The lineup was so universal that it was, until LY 7, not even understood as a tactical choice. It was simply how the round began. Coaches called it "the line." Players called it "lining up." Talia Featherquill, in her LY 6 column on the Foxes' off-season, called it "the only thing in the League that has not been argued about, which is suspicious."</p>
        <p>The argument began, as most League arguments do, in Boise. Pell Whittlestone — who has never coached a team but has the standing to suggest things to teams who do — observed during a Badgers training session that the line was wasteful. Her argument was simple: roles existed. Tanks soaked damage. Healers healed. Snipers picked. If you put all five at the same depth from the enemy spawn, the Tank reached the enemy first by virtue of being faster on the open field, then the Striker, then the Sniper — meaning the role most equipped to absorb the opening volley got there first, and the role least equipped got there last, so by the time the Sniper had a clear lane, the Tank was already engaged at melee range. Featherquill, on hearing this, wrote: "Whittlestone has identified, in one observation, what every coach in the League has chosen not to identify in seven years. The roles should not start at the same depth. The roles should start at the depth their job requires."</p>
        <p>The Boise Badgers ran the first non-line opening at the LY 7 Hallowtide preliminary against the Phoenix Foxes. Bjorn Bristlebeard III moved forward two paces from the line; the Sniper moved back four; the Healer moved back six. The Foxes, lined up as usual, lost the round in fifty-two seconds. The Boise opening was not against any rule — the spawn marks define a region, not a single line within it — and the Commissioner, on review, declined to clarify a rule that had not been written. The Badgers won three more matches that season using variations on the staggered opening. They called it "phalanx." Reporters called it "the Boise opening." Whittlestone, when asked, said: "I called it pre-positioning. The reporters can call it what they like."</p>
        <p>By the LY 8 off-season, every team had been asked privately by their own front office to develop pre-positioning playbooks. The off-season Coaches' Caucus, which traditionally focuses on roster moves and contract disputes, devoted six full sessions to formation theory. The minutes of those sessions are public; the formations that emerged from them are now codified in League broadcast material as "the six." They are: the line (still used as a default and a deliberate "we're not telling you anything" signal), the wedge (Tank at the point, ranged at the back — the inverse of the Boise opening, used by teams confident in a Tank's ability to break the enemy front), the phalanx (Whittlestone's original — three ranks of depth-appropriate roles), the echelon (a diagonal stagger, used when a team wants to angle the engagement away from a particular flank), the killbox (snipers spread wide on flanks, brawlers funnel the enemy to a center where the Tank waits), and the flying-V (Speedsters lead, Tanks flank — the only formation that puts the Tank away from the point of contact, used by teams that want to scout aggressively before committing).</p>
        <p>Reaction across the coaching ranks varies. The Boise Badgers' assistant coach told the Caucus: "I am not happy about this. I was happy with the line. The line was simple. The line was a thing you did and then you fought. Now I have six things I do and then I fight, and the wrong choice in the first thing makes the fight harder." The Otters' coach, by contrast, has been delighted; the Otters were the first team to use all six formations in a single match (R3 LY 8 against the Pandas, who lost the match without ever realizing the Otters were rotating). Wartooth, when asked about the Wolves' formation playbook, said: "We have a playbook. The playbook is one page. The page says: ENGAGE. The Wolves have not adopted the six. The Wolves do not need the six." Subsequent matches have suggested he is, in fact, lying — the Wolves use at least the wedge and the killbox, and a phalanx variant that the League's tactical analysts call "the Wolves' phalanx" because it lacks a Healer at the back rank. The Wolves, of course, do not field a Healer.</p>
        <p>Two formations have been tried and abandoned. Featherquill, in her LY 8 mid-season column, devotes a paragraph to each: "The Beavers attempted, in R5, a formation where every knight stands on the same point. This is not a formation. This is a single knight made of five knights, and it is illegal under Rule 4.2 (knights must occupy distinct positions at the opening bell). The Pandas attempted, in R8, a formation where every knight stands on a different point but all five points are within a four-pace radius. This is technically legal. It is also, on review, not a formation. It is a hug." The Pandas' coach, on being shown Featherquill's paragraph, did not deny the characterization. "It was a hug," he said. "We needed it."</p>
        <p>The League's broadcast team now displays the formation name on the team panel before each round. The names appear in small gold type beneath the team's record. Reno's official statistician, who has been compiling formation-vs-formation outcome rates since the off-season, reported at the LY 8 mid-season Caucus that no formation has yet shown a statistically significant edge against any other, with the possible exception of "anything vs. the hug," which the Pandas have lost every time. The Caucus thanked her for the report and adjourned for lobster.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Coaches' Caucus minutes, LY 8 mid-season summary · "On the Formations Question," compiled for League circulation</div>
      </article>

      <article class="history">
        <h3>Brewer's Reach Gridiron — The Buried Field</h3>
        <div class="meta">A history · Boston Salvage Records, vol. III · LY 8 stadium addition</div>
        <p>The discovery happened during a routine silt-clearing operation on the south bank of the Brewer's Reach in the LY 8 off-season. A Foundation work crew, dredging mud out of the channel below the Pitch, struck stone at a depth their charts said should have been clay. The stone turned out to be a concrete sub-floor. The sub-floor turned out to be a hundred yards long and fifty yards wide. Underneath the sub-floor, the crew found yard markings — the kind of yard markings used in pre-Collapse American football.</p>
        <p>The League's archivists were called in to confirm. The site was a college stadium — small, regional, predating the Collapse by perhaps eighty years. The stadium had been buried, presumably by the same century-long silting that filled the lower Brewer's Reach. The yard lines, painted in a thick latex compound the archivists could not identify, had survived under the silt at full color. The end zones were still tinted faintly yellow. The hash marks were intact. There was, by the archivists' description, "a stadium under the silt that wanted to be a stadium again."</p>
        <p>The Foundation, which had been looking for an additional Boston-area venue since the Pitch began routinely hosting more than its share of bracket matches, made the practical decision: clear the silt, repaint the markings, and add the venue to the bracket rotation. The work took the entire off-season. The Beavers' coaching staff supervised. The Beavers' Tank, on first walking the cleared field, said: "It is a real field. It was a real field. It is a real field again."</p>
        <p>The Gridiron's first official match was the LY 8 R6 Beavers–Otters bracket fixture. The Beavers won. The yard markings, by post-match account, were not used tactically by either team — both teams played their normal openings — but the Otters' coach observed that the lines "made the field feel longer than it is." Featherquill, watching the match from the press box, agreed. "The yard lines are decoration," she wrote, "but they are decoration that tells the eye how far things are. The eye uses the decoration. The match feels like a longer match because of the decoration. This is the first time I have approved of decoration."</p>
        <p>The lobster vendor of Brewer's Reach attended the inaugural match. She set up her booth on the south concourse, sold bisque to a slightly larger crowd than usual, and was overheard, during the third round, telling a paying customer: "I have been here." The customer reportedly said: "At this stadium?" The vendor reportedly said: "Yes. At this stadium." The customer is recorded as not pursuing the question further.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Boston Salvage Records, vol. III, "Stadiums Recovered" · Foundation Atlas Committee</div>
      </article>

      <article class="history">
        <h3>The Atlas Committee's Off-Season</h3>
        <div class="meta">A history · Float Foundation Atlas Committee · LY 8 mid-to-late off-season report</div>
        <p>The Brewer's Reach Gridiron resurrection (LY 8 R6, Beavers–Otters fixture) was, by every internal Foundation account, a financial success. Bracket revenue at the Gridiron exceeded the Pitch's average bracket revenue by twenty-three percent. The Beavers' front office, the LMG broadcast team, the lobster vendor (who set up an additional booth at the new venue), and the Atlas Committee all separately concluded that the league had been leaving money on the field for seven years by hosting every sport at the same handful of venues.</p>
        <p>The proposal that came out of the LY 8 mid-season Atlas Committee meeting was simple in concept and operationally enormous: every city in the League should have a venue for every sport. Twelve cities. Eight sports. Ninety-six stadiums. The Foundation should fund the construction (or, where possible, the resurfacing of an existing structure) over the back half of the off-season. Bracket assignment, currently a rotational schedule constrained by which city had a venue for which sport, would become a genuine open draw — any match could happen anywhere, and the host city would pull the gate.</p>
        <p>The Committee's case was presented to the Coaches' Caucus by Pell Whittlestone, who was nominally not on the Committee but had been doing most of the technical drawings. Her presentation, by the minutes, was forty-seven slides long and lasted three hours. The Wolves' coach left at slide nineteen. The Pandas' coach fell asleep at slide thirty-two and was, by Caucus tradition, not woken up. Featherquill was in the room as a non-voting observer; she wrote a column afterward that remains the cleanest single summary of what the project actually was: <em>"Whittlestone has proposed that every city build every kind of pitch. The reason is that every city already has every kind of fan. The fans of soccer in Phoenix should not have to travel to Olympia to watch soccer. They have been traveling to Olympia for seven years. They are tired of traveling. The fans of curling in Helena should not have to travel to Wichita to watch curling. They have been traveling to Wichita for seven years. The Wolves do not let them sit comfortably. Whittlestone's proposal is that every city's fans, of every sport, should be able to sit comfortably at home. This is a proposal about chairs. It is also, incidentally, a proposal about ninety-six stadiums."</em></p>
        <p>The construction did not, in fact, take ninety-six new buildings. The Committee's survey of existing infrastructure found that most cities already had something — a salvaged gymnasium, a flat industrial floor, a flooded basin that froze in winter — that could host a sport with appropriate markings and surface treatment. The actual new construction was nineteen venues. The remaining seventy-seven were resurfacing projects, line-paint repaintings, and (in three cases) a full re-roofing where the existing structure had been open to the sky and the sport demanded controlled climate. The total off-season cost was eleven thousand bits and a substantial volume of lobster.</p>
        <p>Each city kept its signature surface — Phoenix's red sandstone, Boston's salt-cured grass, Reno's neon-lit synthetic strip, Helena's high-altitude ice, Olympia's riverbed gravel, Wichita's wheatbelt clay, and so on through the twelve. The court markings were laid on top of the signature surface using a paint formulation Whittlestone developed specifically to be visible across all twelve substrates. The result, on inspection, is that every city's stadium for any given sport is visually distinct from every other city's stadium for the same sport: a Phoenix Sandstone Pitch does not look like a Helena Glacier Pitch, even though both are soccer pitches with identical markings. The lines are the same. The lines on top of the surface are the variable. Whittlestone, in her engineering notes for the project, refers to this as "the prefix-and-suffix approach": each venue is a prefix (the city's surface) and a suffix (the sport's markings), and any combination is legitimate.</p>
        <p>Reactions across the league varied. The Foxes' coach approved on the first day; the Foxes had been complaining about the lack of a Phoenix-hosted hardcourt since LY 3. The Otters approved, on the grounds that a soccer pitch in Olympia was overdue. The Wolves declined to comment; their assistant coach, when pressed, said: "We do not need a curling sheet in Wichita. We have a curling sheet in Wichita. The new one is also a curling sheet. We do not see what is different." The Wolves' new curling sheet is, by the Atlas Committee's records, identical in markings to the old one but installed on the wheatbelt clay surface rather than the original Wichita Underdig stone. The Wolves use both. The Wolves win at both. The Wolves do not appear to notice which is which.</p>
        <p>The lobster vendor of Brewer's Reach was offered, at the off-season's end, a contract by the Foundation to run a booth at any venue she liked. She declined. She has, however, been documented at thirty-one of the new ninety-six venues at one match each across the LY 8 second half — never her Brewer's Reach booth, always a different vendor's booth where she sat on the patron side of the counter, ate one bowl of bisque, and left without comment. Foundation officials, on noticing the pattern, did not interfere. Featherquill, on noticing the pattern, wrote a single sentence about it in her column: <em>"The lobster vendor is taking a tour. She is not telling us what she thinks of any of it. We will know when she has finished."</em></p>
        <p>The complete Atlas, including all 96 city-by-sport venues plus the seventeen retained legacy and special-event venues (the Foundation Hall, the Float Gas Foundry, the Hallowtide Hollow, the Sleepless Forest, and the others that don't belong to a single city's home rotation), is now the canonical bracket-assignment pool. Any match in any week can be drawn for any of the 113 venues. Featherquill's working theory, which she has not yet committed to print, is that within three more seasons the League will have venues for sports that have not yet been invented. She has refused, when asked, to guess what those sports might be.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Float Foundation Atlas Committee, "The Ninety-Six Project" · LY 8 off-season summary report</div>
      </article>

      <article class="history">
        <h3>The Effect Overlays</h3>
        <div class="meta">A history · Float Foundation Atlas Committee, addendum 2 · LY 8 off-season closeout</div>
        <p>The Ninety-Six Project shipped in time for the second half of LY 8 — twelve cities, eight sports, ninety-six city-by-sport venues, all on the bracket-assignment pool. Within four bracket weeks, the Atlas Committee received correspondence asking the same question from three separate parties (the LMG broadcast team, the Foxes' merchandising office, and the lobster vendor of Brewer's Reach, who is not technically a party but whose questions the Foundation answers anyway): the new venues looked great, but they all looked the same on a given day. Was there a way to dress them up?</p>
        <p>The objection was specific. A Phoenix Sandstone Pitch looks fine on a sunny afternoon. It also looks fine on every other afternoon. The Reno Neon Hardcourt has its strip lights running every match. The Helena Glacier Rink shows snowfall every match. There was no day-to-day variation within the same venue. Pell Whittlestone, presented with this complaint by Featherquill at the LY 8 mid-late off-season Caucus, considered it for a long moment and said: "They want weather."</p>
        <p>What Whittlestone produced over the next two weeks was not weather, exactly. It was a system of <em>effect overlays</em> — stackable thematic re-skins that any venue could host on any given match. The catalog she presented to the Caucus contained nine overlays, deliberately scoped to be visually distinct from one another while leaving the underlying surface and markings recognizable. They are: <em>Stock</em> (no overlay — the venue's natural state), <em>Foundry</em> (heat radiating from below, embers drifting up, scorched flooring), <em>Underdig</em> (the venue is enclosed in rock, lit by mineral phosphorescence, water dripping somewhere out of frame), <em>Submerged</em> (the entire venue is shallow tide-pool, with bubbles rising and seaweed swaying), <em>Nightwoods</em> (forested, fireflies drifting overhead, mushrooms clustered along the perimeter), <em>Hallowtide</em> (themed for the Reno Hallowtide week — bats overhead, pumpkins, gravestones), <em>Petalfall</em> (continuous cherry-petal drift across the surface), <em>Frostfall</em> (light snow accumulating around markings), and <em>Neonspark</em> (UV-reactive paint, sparks discharging from underfield generators between rounds). Three more — <em>Bramble</em> (overgrown reclamation, vines climbing the markings), <em>Aurora</em> (high-latitude light show, color-shifted crystals along the perimeter), and <em>Harvest</em> (late-autumn gourds and wheat-stalks, falling cherry leaves) — were added in a follow-up after the initial nine landed. The catalog stands at twelve as of LY 8 closeout.</p>
        <p>The math, by the Caucus' arithmetic, was: 12 cities × 8 sports × 12 overlays = 1,152 visually distinct venue configurations. The actual physical inventory was unchanged — there are still 96 buildings — but each one can now host any of twelve looks depending on which the bracket schedule (or the host city's mood) draws. Whittlestone's engineering notes describe the overlays as "the third axis of the prefix-and-suffix model": each venue is a prefix (city) plus a midfix (effect) plus a suffix (sport markings). Any combination is legitimate. Most are unprecedented.</p>
        <p>Reactions, again, varied. The Foxes' coach was delighted to learn that "Phoenix Sandstone Foundry Hardcourt" was now a thing the calendar could produce — the Foxes lean thematically toward heat and would, if allowed, host every home match in Foundry overlay. The Pandas' coach asked, in writing, whether there was a way to opt out of the Hallowtide overlay specifically, on the grounds that the Pandas find bats unsettling. He was told, also in writing, by the Commissioner, that there was not. The Wolves' coach declined to comment a second time but was photographed touring a Wichita Wheat Belt Underdig Sheet at sunset, alone, by a freelance Reno reporter who refused to confirm or deny whether Wartooth had been smiling.</p>
        <p>The Atlas Committee's online preview tool — accessible through the Lore tab's Stadiums section — shows the 96 city-by-sport venues at their default Stock overlay by default, with a picker bar of all twelve overlays at the top of the page. Selecting an overlay re-renders the entire 96-card grid with that effect applied, so users can browse what (e.g.) every Hallowtide variant of every venue looks like. The Committee's stated reason for this presentation choice is that 1,152 cards on one page is, in their phrasing, "rude to the eye." Featherquill's column for the launch week endorsed the picker design and added the only criticism that has stuck: <em>"The picker is correct. The catalog is correct. The naming convention — 'Phoenix Sandstone Foundry Court' — is also correct, but it is becoming difficult to say out loud during a broadcast. The LMG anchors have asked, off the record, if the venues could be given short names. The Atlas Committee has refused. The Atlas Committee believes the long names are accurate. The Atlas Committee is not wrong. The Atlas Committee is also not the one having to read these names aloud at the top of the hour. We will see who blinks first."</em></p>
        <p>As of the LY 8 closeout, neither party has blinked. The long names are official. The short names exist informally among the broadcast team. The lobster vendor's tour through the venues, which was at thirty-one venues at the end of the previous addendum, is now at sixty-three — every Stock-overlay venue she had not yet visited, plus a small set of Foundry, Submerged, and Nightwoods variants she has reportedly chosen by some criterion no one can determine. Foundation officials still do not interfere. Featherquill still has not committed her working theory to print. The lobster vendor has, by the most recent count, eaten one bowl of bisque per visit. She has at no point ordered a second.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Float Foundation Atlas Committee, "Effect Overlays — Ninety-Six Project addendum 2" · LY 8 off-season closeout</div>
      </article>

      <article class="history">
        <h3>On Broadcasting the Setup</h3>
        <div class="meta">A history · LMG technical bulletin no. 51 · Treadwell, supervising · LY 8 R10</div>
        <p>The Coaches' Caucus codified the six formations during the LY 8 off-season. The bracket schedule began running formations live in R1. By R5, every broadcast had a moment in the opening sequence where the camera would pan across one team's pre-positioning, then across the other's, while the play-by-play voice would say <em>something like</em> "Beavers in their phalanx... Otters showing the wedge..." but not always those exact words, because the play-by-play voice was guessing — the broadcast booth had no actual data on which formation each team had drawn.</p>
        <p>The booth was guessing because the formation was a coaching decision made on the bench in the seconds between the previous round's resolution and the next round's opening bell. The booth had no microphone on the bench (League rule, codified LY 1, Old Bristlebeard the First's only personal contribution to the rulebook: <em>"What the coaches say to the knights is between the coaches and the knights"</em>). The booth could only watch the formation resolve on the field after the bell, identify it visually, and announce it then. By the time the booth announced it, the round was already in motion. The audience missed the setup.</p>
        <p>Treadwell raised this with the Caucus in R8. Her proposal was specific and technically simple: the Caucus's record of each round's formation choice — which already existed on the bench's side, on a small card the assistant coach handed to the field marshal at the bell — would be transmitted to the booth as a small data line, fifteen seconds before the bell. The booth would then surface the formation pair to the audience as part of the round-strip readout above the play field. <em>"The audience is not asking us to predict the round,"</em> Treadwell wrote. <em>"The audience is asking us to give them what the coaches already know. We are denying them an honest piece of information that is already, somewhere on the bench, written down on a card."</em></p>
        <p>The Caucus debated the proposal for two sessions. The Wolves' coach, predictably, opposed it. His written argument, filed with the Caucus secretary: <em>"What the Wolves have drawn is the Wolves' business. What the Pandas have drawn is the Pandas' business. The audience is welcome to watch the round and identify it themselves, like real fans."</em> The Pandas' coach, less predictably, also opposed it — for the opposite reason: <em>"If the booth tells the audience we have drawn the hug, the audience will laugh at us before the round starts. We would prefer the audience laugh at us after the round, when the round has earned the laugh."</em> Featherquill, in her column on the debate, observed: <em>"The Wolves want secrecy. The Pandas want dignity. These are not the same thing. Treadwell wants information. Information is more honest than secrecy and kinder than dignity. Treadwell will win."</em></p>
        <p>The vote, by the Caucus minutes, was nine in favor and three opposed (Wolves, Pandas, and — quietly — the Foxes, whose coach declined to explain his vote). The proposal passed in R10. The round-strip readout has, since R10, included each team's formation in parentheses next to the team code: <em>BVR (PHALANX) vs (WEDGE) OTR</em>. The Wolves continue to protest. The Pandas have stopped protesting after Featherquill's mid-season column noted, parenthetically, that the Pandas had not actually drawn the hug since R6 and that the audience had been kindly waiting for them to recover from it.</p>
        <p>Treadwell, on being asked whether she was satisfied with the change, said: <em>"It is what should have been there from the beginning. It is not satisfaction. It is correction."</em> The technical bulletin, like all Treadwell bulletins, closes with her standard signoff: <em>"The audience owns the moment. We frame it for them."</em></p>
        <div class="signoff">— LMG Technical Bulletin no. 51, "Formation Broadcast Surface" · Treadwell, supervising · LY 8 R10</div>
      </article>

      <article class="history">
        <h3>The Four-Team Expansion</h3>
        <div class="meta">A history · Office of the Commissioner · LY 8 closeout, ratified for the LY 9 founding bell</div>
        <p>The League had run, for eight seasons, on twelve teams from twelve cities. The number was not by design — it was by application. Eleven cities had asked to play in LY 1; Helena's Hedgehogs had walked in late, in LY 7, with a complete roster and a notarized request, and the League had welcomed them. Four other settlements had asked, across various seasons, and been politely deferred for reasons that were always specific and never about them. The Bayou settlement had been told the League was "still calibrating its travel logistics for the deep south." The Anchorage settlement had been told the League was "considering, with care, the welfare of teams asked to travel from the far north." The Denver settlement had been told the League had "no current opening." The Pittsburgh settlement had been told nothing at all, as their original application appeared to have been lost in transit by the Boston post-office's third-quarter sort, an incident the post-office still maintains was not their fault.</p>
        <p>The Bayou Tigers, the Anchorage Polar Bears, the Denver Bison, and the Pittsburgh Ravens reapplied jointly in the LY 8 mid-season. Their joint application is on record at the Office of the Commissioner. It is two pages long. It opens: <em>"We have waited. We have read the rules. We are ready. We bring four cities, four rosters, four crowds, and one shared question, which is: what would we have to do to be welcome."</em> The remainder is rosters and travel logistics. The signatures, in order, are: a Tiger matriarch named Ojee Stripeshoulder, a Polar Bear elder named Tamzen Whitepaw, a Bison patriarch named Yellowhide of the Denver herd, and a Raven captain named Quorrik (no patronymic recorded — Ravens, by tradition, only take their parent's name after their first kill in defense of the murder, and Quorrik had not yet had occasion).</p>
        <p>Commissioner Whiskerstone read the application twice, asked her chief of staff to confirm the rosters and travel arrangements were real (they were), and called a Coaches' Caucus emergency session. The Caucus debate ran four sessions over two days. The objections, by the secretary's minutes, fell into three rough buckets: <em>logistics</em> (could the schedule absorb four new teams without the season running long), <em>parity</em> (had the new teams played enough rounds in any organized format to be expected to compete), and <em>tradition</em> (the League was twelve teams; it had always been twelve teams; "always" being seven full seasons). The Tigers' representative, attending as observer, declined to comment on logistics or parity. On tradition she said: <em>"The League is eight years old. Tradition is forty-eight."</em> The Caucus secretary recorded that several coaches laughed; the secretary did not specify which.</p>
        <p>The vote was nine to three in favor of admitting all four teams jointly, effective the LY 9 founding bell. The three opposed were Wartooth (Wolves, on logistics), the Pandas' coach (parity), and the Sharks' coach (declined to explain his vote, but the Sharks' assistant coach later told the Wing Standard that the Sharks were nervous about the Tigers as a stylistic competitor and the captain "did not want a stylistic competitor.") Whiskerstone, in her ratification statement, wrote: <em>"The League will be sixteen teams from the LY 9 founding bell. Sixteen is a number we are choosing. It is not a fixed number. The next time someone walks the distance from somewhere we have not heard of and shows up at Brewer's Reach with a roster, we will read their application, and we will choose again."</em></p>
        <p>The bracket schedule, beginning LY 9, draws eight of the sixteen teams at random per season for the standard quarterfinal-through-final ladder; the other eight sit out and prepare. Ranked teams (by championships, then by wins) are not given byes — every team enters the random draw with equal weight. The Wolves' coach, on hearing this, filed a written objection (still pending). The Polar Bears' representative, on hearing this, sent the Office of the Commissioner a single-line response: <em>"We will play the matches we are drawn for. We will be ready for any of them."</em> The Office filed the response. It is the shortest piece of inter-team correspondence currently on file.</p>
        <p>Featherquill's column for the announcement week closed with what is now widely quoted in the Lore tab and on League merchandise: <em>"Sixteen is more teams than I can comfortably profile in a single season. I will profile them anyway. The League just got more interesting. Sometimes 'more interesting' is the only good news the season has."</em></p>
        <div class="signoff">— Office of the Commissioner, "Ratification of the Four-Team Expansion" · LY 8 closeout</div>
      </article>

      <article class="history">
        <h3>The Continental Expansion</h3>
        <div class="meta">A history · Geography Caucus addendum · LY 9 mid-season, ratified at the founding bell</div>
        <p>The League had, by the LY 8 closeout, sixteen teams from sixteen cities. The cities were all from the same large region of the former North American continent — the region the Old World had labeled "the United States of America." The label had not been re-applied after the Collapse, but the boundary had, by tacit consensus, continued to define where the League looked for new applicants. The Geography Caucus had not been asked to formalize this. The Geography Caucus had not been asked to do anything in particular for several seasons. The Geography Caucus consisted, in LY 8, of one Owl and a stack of pre-Collapse maps.</p>
        <p>The reclassification was triggered by an envelope. The envelope arrived at the Office of the Commissioner in the LY 9 mid-season, postmarked from a city the Office had no record of accepting mail from: a place the runes called "Whitehorse," several days' travel north of Anchorage. The envelope contained a single document, signed by an organization that called itself the Yukon Boreal Council, asking on behalf of the Yukon Hunting Lynx whether the League considered "the geographic boundary of the former United States of America" to be a binding constraint on its recruiting, or whether the League considered <em>the post-Collapse continent</em> — the entire former North America — to be the relevant frame.</p>
        <p>Whiskerstone read the document twice. Whiskerstone forwarded the document to the Geography Caucus with a single handwritten line: <em>"Owl, please advise."</em> The Owl, whose name is Pellic Snowcrest and who has been the sole member of the Geography Caucus since LY 4 because no one else had asked to join, took three days. She returned a memo of approximately one paragraph. The memo opens: <em>"The boundary the Old World drew across the continent does not exist anymore. It has not existed for nine years. The boundary the League has been operating under is a hand-me-down boundary. We have been recruiting under the assumption that what is now southern Alaska is in our recruiting area but what is now the Yukon is not, even though the only difference between the two is a line on a map that no longer exists. This is not a coherent position. We should adopt a coherent position."</em> The memo's recommendation, in its second and final paragraph: that the recruiting boundary be redefined as <em>"the former North American continent, in its entirety, including the regions the Old World labeled Canada and Mexico."</em> The Caucus's recommendation passed Coaches' Caucus on the first vote.</p>
        <p>The notification went out — formally, with stamped seals from the Office of the Commissioner — to every animal community on the continent that had ever submitted an application to the League and been informed there was no opening. Three responses arrived within a fortnight. The Quebec Crashing Moose, who had applied in LY 5 and been told (politely) that the League's logistics could not accommodate teams from "outside the recruiting area." The Sonora Sly Coyotes, who had applied in LY 6 and been told the same. The Yucatan Vibrant Quetzals, who had applied in LY 7 and been told the same. All three asked, in carefully phrased letters, whether the League was now in a position to reconsider. The Lynx, by the same mail, asked the same question. The Office's response to each of the four was identical: <em>"Yes. We were wrong. Please send your roster."</em></p>
        <p>The four delegations arrived at Brewer's Reach over the course of the LY 9 mid-season. The Lynx came first — a single Lynx coach, alone, carrying the rosters and credentials of all five active knights, because the Lynx had decided, in their own way, that this was a one-Lynx job. The Moose came second — three Moose, by river, having floated down the Saint-Laurent system to the coast and then walked overland the rest of the way. The Coyotes came third — six Coyotes, in a small caravan, having taken a route north through the Sonora desert and east through the former Texas grassland that the Bison's coaching staff later certified was indeed the most efficient line. The Quetzals came last — by air, in a fortnight of relay flights with rest stops the Quetzals selected based on what trees the Old World had not chopped down. All four delegations were welcomed. All four were inducted at the LY 9 founding bell.</p>
        <p>Featherquill's column for the founding-bell announcement read, in part: <em>"The League is now twenty teams across the former North American continent. It is not the United States of America's league. It was never the United States of America's league. The United States of America does not exist. The League exists. The League is twenty teams now and it is, in my honest professional opinion, the most interesting it has ever been. I am tired. I am also a journalist. The two facts are not, on close inspection, entirely separable."</em></p>
        <p>The bracket schedule, beginning LY 9 mid-season, draws twelve of the twenty teams at random per season for the standard qualifier-then-eight-team-ladder format. The other eight sit out and prepare. Ranked teams continue to receive byes through to the quarterfinals; the qualifier round has expanded to four matches accordingly. The Lynx coach, on being told the format, asked only one question: whether the qualifier matches counted. The Office of the Commissioner confirmed that they did. The Lynx coach nodded, said "good," and left to find a place to eat. The Office's chief of staff has reported that, as of the most recent count, no one in the Office has yet figured out where the Lynx coach went to eat or whether they ever returned to their lodging.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Geography Caucus addendum (Snowcrest) · Office of the Commissioner, "Ratification of the Continental Expansion" · LY 9 founding bell</div>
      </article>

      <article class="history">
        <h3>The Commitment Directive</h3>
        <div class="meta">A history · Coaches' Caucus pre-season working session · LY 9 training camp, ratified before the founding bell</div>
        <p>The complaint had been growing for several seasons. It came from coaches first, then from broadcast analysts, then — once Featherquill picked it up in her LY 8 closeout column — from the audience. The complaint was: <em>knights, in the middle stretch of any round, jiggle</em>. They take a step left. They take a step right. They take another step left. They do not, by any close reading of the footage, appear to be moving toward anything. They are not moving away from anything either. They are simply standing somewhere they cannot quite commit to leaving and cannot quite commit to staying.</p>
        <p>The phenomenon had several names across the League. The Beavers' coaching staff called it <em>the dither</em>. The Sharks called it <em>the indecision</em>. The Pandas, with characteristic dry self-awareness, called it <em>the standard mid-round Panda</em>. The Wolves did not call it anything because the Wolves' assistant coach refused to acknowledge that the Wolves did it, although film analysis of the LY 8 R7 Wolves–Otters fixture had clearly shown three Wolves doing it simultaneously across one twenty-second window. The Wolves' assistant coach, when shown the footage, said: <em>"Those Wolves were guarding."</em> The film analyst, in a follow-up interview, said: <em>"They were not guarding. They were dithering."</em> Both descriptions were entered into the record.</p>
        <p>The diagnosis came from a sniper named Korvik who fights for the Ravens. Korvik wrote a single-page paper for the Caucus's pre-season working session. The paper opens: <em>"A knight on the field has, at any moment, a target it could close on, a teammate it could support, a position it could hold, or a corner it could refuse. The dither is what happens when the knight has access to all four options and has not yet committed to any of them. The dither does not end on its own. The knight has to be told: pick one. Hold it. Reconsider in a moment. Not now."</em> The paper is, by Caucus archivist Kirrin's note, the shortest tactical contribution ever submitted to the working session. It is also the most circulated.</p>
        <p>The Caucus, working with Pell Whittlestone, formalized Korvik's diagnosis into the <em>Commitment Directive</em>: a coaching standard agreed across all sixteen teams' training programs for LY 9. Its three rules, as printed on every team's LY 9 training-camp wall:</p>
        <p><em>1. Commit to a heading for at least eight strides before reconsidering, except in three cases: your target died, your target crossed into melee range, or you have been told to redirect by your bench.</em></p>
