        <p><em>2. When you have no target, hold a position. The position is your guard. Do not drift. Pick the position once, hold it for sixty strides, and only then reconsider where to stand. The team will rotate around its anchors. You may be an anchor. Behave like one.</em></p>
        <p><em>3. When two of you are closing on the same enemy, do not push each other off-line. Trust the side-by-side. The side-by-side is faster than the wide arc.</em></p>
        <p>The directive went into effect at the LY 9 training camps. By the third week of camp, every team had adopted the language. By the fourth week, the dither had measurably decreased — Whittlestone's training-camp tracking, conducted with stopwatches and grid-paper across two hundred sample rounds, showed that <em>per-knight directional reversals dropped from an average of 1.4 per ten strides to 0.3</em>. Whittlestone, in her closing-week presentation, called the result "the largest single-season behavioral change in League knight conduct since the Float Gas Foundry's first delivery, which was admittedly a chemical change rather than a behavioral one." The Caucus laughed, by the secretary's note, harder than the joke really merited; the secretary attributed the laughter to relief.</p>
        <p>The Polar Bears, in their first training camp as a League team, adopted the directive on day one and asked whether it had a name in their language. Whitepaw answered for the team: <em>"In our language, this is just walking. We have always done this. We were not aware the other teams did not."</em> The Caucus, by all accounts, did not laugh at this. The Polar Bears were not making a joke.</p>
        <p>Featherquill's closing column on the directive: <em>"The League has, for eight seasons, been teaching its knights how to fight. It has not been teaching them how to walk. The Polar Bears walked thirty-two days in the snow to remind us. The Bayou Tigers stalk for a reason. The Bison stand because someone has to. The Ravens commit because Korvik wrote one good page. We are about to watch a much better League. I am, for the first time in three seasons, not certain who is going to win the Coronation Cup. This is the best feeling sportswriting offers."</em></p>
        <div class="signoff">— Coaches' Caucus, "The Commitment Directive — pre-season working session minutes" · LY 9 training camp, week four</div>
      </article>
    </div>

    <!-- ===== STADIUMS ===== -->
    <!-- v28.13: Stadium factory now spans 20 cities × 8 sports × 12 effects =
         1,920 venue configurations across 160 physical buildings. The Lore
         page shows the 160 "Stock" stadiums by default; a picker bar lets
         users browse the 160 variants of any chosen effect overlay. -->
    <div class="lore-section" data-loresection="stadiums">
      <article class="history">
        <h3>The League's Stadium Atlas</h3>
        <div class="meta">A field guide · Float Foundation Atlas Committee · live previews</div>
        <p>The complete Atlas, as of the LY 9 founding bell: 20 cities across the former North American continent, 8 sports, 12 effect overlays — 1,920 venue configurations across 160 physical buildings. Each card shows a faithful preview of the surface, its court markings, and (when an overlay is selected) the thematic effect applied. The picker bar at the top of the grid lets you browse every variant of every overlay; "Stock" shows the 160 venues in their natural state, while the other eleven effects re-skin them with their thematic dressing.</p>
        <p>The naming convention follows Whittlestone's three-axis "prefix-and-suffix model": <em>{City} {Adjective} [{Effect}] {Sport Venue}</em>. So a Phoenix soccer pitch with the Foundry overlay reads "Phoenix Sandstone Foundry Pitch"; a Reno tennis court at default reads "Reno Neon Court"; a Helena ice rink with Hallowtide overlay reads "Helena Glacier Hallowtide Rink"; a Yucatan badminton hall with Petalfall reads "Yucatan Jungle-Canopy Petalfall Net Hall." The names are long. The names are also accurate. The Quebec Crashing Moose's home Saint-Laurent venue is, by its own translator, "the only venue in the League whose name reads correctly in both League Common and pre-Collapse French."</p>
      </article>
      <div id="loreStadiumGrid"></div>
    </div>

    <!-- ===== BIOGRAPHIES ===== -->
    <div class="lore-section" data-loresection="bios">
      <article class="bio">
        <h3>Commissioner Margaux "Fang" Whiskerstone</h3>
        <div class="meta">Maine Coon · b. uncertain · League Office, head</div>
        <p>The Commissioner has been the Commissioner since League Year 4. Before that, the Commissioner was, in order: a port-authority arbitration officer in Boston, a syndicated columnist in Tampa, the proprietor of a moderately successful tea shop in Olympia, and (briefly) a competitive sleeper. None of this should be relevant. All of it has come up.</p>
        <p>Whiskerstone is famous for her dispute resolution method, which is to physically sit on the contested object until both parties agree to a settlement. This works because the Commissioner is a Maine Coon, and Maine Coons are extraordinarily heavy when they want to be. The Office has documented at least two cases where she sat on a stadium's title deed for the better part of a fortnight before either city blinked.</p>
        <p>In a rare interview, the Commissioner once said: "I do not enforce the rules. I am the rules. Please do not test this." The interviewer's follow-up question is recorded in the League archives only as "[laughter]."</p>
        <p>Her chair is famously raised on a small dais. The League Office insists this is "for visibility." Everyone in the Office knows it is because Maine Coons hate not being the tallest creature in the room.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— "Who's Who in the League," updated annually</div>
      </article>

      <article class="bio">
        <h3>Old Bristlebeard the First</h3>
        <div class="meta">Beaver · Tank · BVR · in the Hall · died LY 5 R5</div>
        <p>The first martyr. The first true Beaver. The patriarch of a name that has now been retired twice and re-fielded three times.</p>
        <p>Old Bristlebeard the First was, by all accounts, slow. He moved at the speed of an unloaded freight wagon. He was the worst Tank in his rookie cohort by the metric of footwork; he was the best by the metric of "still standing at the end of the round." He led the Beavers in damage absorbed for five consecutive seasons, never won a single match's MVP award, and did not seem to mind.</p>
        <p>He died in the championship final of Season 5, holding a position that did not need to be held. He took three Beavers with him in the blast — a friendly-fire call that was, in his case, a strategic call: he ate the grenade so the Wolves could not use it on his squad. The League has classed it as <em>tactical self-sacrifice</em> and the Wolves still complain about it.</p>
        <p>His Riot Shield is the most-traveled item in League history. His Plasma Mace returned to a Bristlebeard exactly once, for one round, before being lost in the same match.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Hall of Fallen Heroes plaque, BVR</div>
      </article>

      <article class="bio">
        <h3>Vela Hootington</h3>
        <div class="meta">Owl · Speed · OWL · in the Hall · died LY 8 R7</div>
        <p>The fastest Owl to ever hold a weapon. Vela Hootington's 22-kill streak still stands; the next two knights to break it both died trying.</p>
        <p>Hootington was a paradox. She moved like fog and spoke like she had a clock running. Her teammates loved her and resented her in roughly equal proportion, because she would lap them mid-engagement, and because she would refuse to repeat instructions she'd already given. She was famous for two pieces of equipment: her Plate Cuirass, which she handed to a teammate for a single round (her last) and which has since become known as Vela's Plate Cuirass, and her Stun Baton, which her killer (a Shark named Tomik Splashfang) carried for the rest of his career and then died holding.</p>
        <p>The "Vela's Plate Cuirass" item — the legacy item, in scavenge circulation — is currently believed to be on a knight in Olympia, though that knight has refused to confirm. The teammate she gave it to never spoke about her again. He retired after that season and bought the tea shop the Commissioner used to own. He still works there.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Owl Hall, Sundip Memorial Wing</div>
      </article>

      <article class="bio">
        <h3>Mira Pawington, "The Scavenger Queen"</h3>
        <div class="meta">Kit · Speed · KIT · in the Hall · died LY 5 R6</div>
        <p>Mira Pawington holds the league record for scavenges in a career: eleven. She held the record for scavenges in a single match (four) until a Raccoon broke it; she does not particularly mind.</p>
        <p>She specialized in <em>polite</em> scavenging. Pawington was not a thief. She would walk up to a fallen knight, kneel, ask the body's permission (a Kits tradition), and then take the gear. League officials documented this in Season 4, when an opposing knight watching from the bench claimed Pawington had spent a full eight seconds in conversation with a corpse. The bench knight insisted, on record, that the corpse <em>responded</em>. The bench knight was sent home.</p>
        <p>Pawington died with her opponent's Power Cell in her hand, two animation frames from completing the steal. The opponent was a Raccoon. The Raccoon survived the round. The Raccoon kept the Power Cell. The Raccoon did not apologize. The Kits hold a moment of polite silence for Pawington in their pre-season retreat every year, and the Raccoons hold a moment of slightly less polite silence at the same time.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— "Quiet Hands: A Memoir of Scavenging," posthumous</div>
      </article>

      <article class="bio">
        <h3>Felix Marshmallow</h3>
        <div class="meta">Panda · Tank · PND · in the Hall · grenade-safety drill namesake · died LY 7 R5</div>
        <p>Felix Marshmallow was the kind of teammate every team wants. He brought baked goods to practice. He learned everyone's siblings' names. He was famous for being the slowest Panda on a roster of slow Pandas, and proud of it.</p>
        <p>He died in Season 7, Round 5, of a fragmentation grenade thrown by a teammate from approximately seven feet away. The teammate had been aiming at an Otter. The Otter was, in fairness, on the other side of Felix Marshmallow.</p>
        <p>The League's grenade safety drills are now mandatory in pre-season for all teams. They were not mandatory before Season 7. The drills are, on the books, called the "Marshmallow Protocol." On the field they are called "Felix Drills." The teammate who threw the grenade has refused to grenade since. He has refused to grenade so emphatically that the Pandas now have a roster carve-out for him: he is <em>not allowed</em> to handle explosives, and his contract specifies it.</p>
        <p>Felix Marshmallow's family receives a benefit from the Float Foundation. The benefit is paid in lobster, at his family's request, sourced from Boston.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Pandas Roster Memorial, Portland Greenhouse</div>
      </article>

      <article class="bio">
        <h3>"Splinter" Tooth (allegedly)</h3>
        <div class="meta">Beaver · disputed historical figure · "the Balloon's Father"</div>
        <p>Did Splinter Tooth exist? No one alive can confirm.</p>
        <p>League folklore credits Splinter Tooth with proposing the Balloon Contract in the League's first year — three balloons, three lives, popped publicly so the scoreboard could be read by the audience. The story is repeated by every Beaver coach to every Beaver rookie. The story has minor variations. In some versions Splinter Tooth was a champion who later retired to the lobster trade. In others, Splinter Tooth was a drunk who proposed the system on a bet and then forgot. In one version, recorded by a Reno historian who is not trusted, Splinter Tooth was a <em>Wolf</em> who infiltrated the Beavers' first roster.</p>
        <p>No League roster from Year 1 lists a "Splinter Tooth." This is taken, by Beavers, as proof of how good Splinter was. It is taken, by everyone else, as proof that Splinter Tooth never existed. Both interpretations agree that the Balloon Contract is real, valid, and binding.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— "Founding Myths," League Library Reading Room</div>
      </article>

      <article class="bio">
        <h3>Hester Quillstrike</h3>
        <div class="meta">Hedgehog · Sniper · HED · in the Hall · died LY 8 R3 · 412-pace record co-holder</div>
        <p>Hester Quillstrike was a finished sniper before her League career began. She grew up shooting at painted plate targets in the foothills of Helena, where Hedgehog kids learn ranged combat the way Boston kids learn to use a hammer — quietly, daily, without ceremony. By the time HED was admitted to the League in LY 8, Hester was already, in any meaningful sense, the best sniper alive.</p>
        <p>She debuted in HED's inaugural season at the Helena Glacier Rink. Her first League kill was a 290-pace shot through a Wolf's helmet visor in Round 2 of HED's opening match. Three matches later, in HED's third match, she made a 412-pace rail shot across the full diagonal of the Rink, into a fleeing Otter Speedster. The League officials sent calipers to verify the distance. They confirmed. They apologized for asking. Hester said nothing.</p>
        <p>In Round 3 of that same match — the match where she set the record — she was killed by a coordinated four-Otter rush who had clearly identified her as the threat. She fell at her sniper position, her quiver still half-full, her Railgun Rifle still in her hand. The Rifle was scavenged by a Sundip Hootington Jr. shortly afterward. Sundip went on to match her record exactly, posthumously, before his own death at the Reno Open Court later in LY 8.</p>
        <p>The 412-pace mark is now co-held by Hester and Sundip. Neither one is alive to claim it. The Hedgehogs consider this fitting.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Hall of Fallen Heroes, Helena annex</div>
      </article>

      <article class="bio">
        <h3>Tomik Splashfang the Bold</h3>
        <div class="meta">Shark · Brawl · SHK · in the Hall · died LY 3 R2 · grenade-fumble pioneer</div>
        <p>Tomik Splashfang was the kind of Brawler the league used to have — heavy, loud, charging, and immune to any form of strategic thinking that lasted longer than four seconds. He was the captain of the Sharks' inaugural roster (SHK was founded LY 3) and held that role until his death later that same year.</p>
        <p>His final match was Round 2 of SHK's third game ever. The Sharks were up 2-0 in rounds against the Beavers. Tomik had picked up a Frag Grenade — possibly his own, possibly Bristlebeard the First's, accounts differ — and was preparing to throw it at the Beavers' back zone. He was, at the time, two seconds into a stun applied by a Beaver Sniper. The stun ran out exactly when his throw arm would have completed.</p>
        <p>It did not complete. The grenade exploded in his hand. He took two Beavers with him; he also took himself. The Sharks, in fairness, won that match anyway, but they retired Tomik's number for the season and required all Brawlers to undergo a one-week stun-recovery course before handling explosives. The Marshmallow Protocol of LY 7 cites the Splashfang Incident in its preamble.</p>
        <p>Tomik's Stun Baton entered circulation after his death and was carried by, in order, Brask Ironclaw III and Vela Hootington. Vela died holding it. The Stun Baton is now believed to be in private retirement, somewhere on the Boise mountain pass.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Hall of Fallen Heroes, Tampa wing</div>
      </article>

      <article class="bio">
        <h3>Crash Wartooth</h3>
        <div class="meta">Wolf · Brawl · WLF · in the Hall · died LY 6 R3 · Crash Rule namesake</div>
        <p>Crash Wartooth was, by Wolf standards, average. By every other team's standards he was terrifying. The Wolves are a hard grading curve.</p>
        <p>He played five seasons as the Wolves' lead Brawler, accruing 41 kills and an 11-kill streak. His pack-coordination instinct was excellent — the Wolves under his roster had a 70% match-win rate during his tenure. He was not personally famous; he wasn't a face-of-the-team kind of knight. He was reliable in the way load-bearing beams are reliable, and the Wolves liked him for it.</p>
        <p>He died at the Brewer's Reach Pitch in Round 3 of a Wolves vs. Beavers match in LY 6. The Beavers had brought three Stun Batons. Crash, doing what Brawlers do, charged. The Beavers stunned him. Crash stayed standing through the first stun (Wolves are tough). The Beavers stunned him again. Crash stayed standing through the second. The Beavers stunned him a third time. Crash, by this point, was unable to defend himself. A Beaver Brawler closed the kill. The full sequence, including all three stuns, took twelve seconds.</p>
        <p>The Wolves spent the next month re-engineering their Brawler kit. The "Crash Rule" — heavy plate is mandatory for any Wolves Brawler — was adopted in pre-season of LY 7 and has been honored ever since. No Wolf Brawler has died to a stun-volley since the Rule was adopted. Three Stun Batons have been thrown at Wolf Brawlers and bounced off without effect; the Beavers consider this insulting and the Wolves consider it correct.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Hall of Fallen Heroes, Wichita wing</div>
      </article>

      <article class="bio">
        <h3>Sundip Hootington Jr.</h3>
        <div class="meta">Owl · Sniper · OWL · in the Hall · died LY 8 R8 · 412-pace record co-holder</div>
        <p>Sundip Hootington Jr. was the son of Sundip Hootington Sr., who was a journeyman Owl Brawler who died unspectacularly in his fourth season. The Junior was, by the family's accounting, supposed to be a Brawler too. He was not. He was a Sniper. The family did not approve until he tied the league's longest-shot record posthumously, by which time the family was no longer in a position to disapprove.</p>
        <p>His career was sparse and excellent. 36 kills across 4 seasons, 13-kill streak, 4 scavenges. He was famously methodical, disliked by his own teammates for refusing to call shots in advance, and feared by Healers for the obvious reason. He took his job seriously enough to make weekly visits to the Hall of Fallen Heroes' Hester Quillstrike memorial — he had carried her Railgun Rifle for two seasons after her death and considered it a debt.</p>
        <p>His final match was at the Reno Open Court in LY 8. He fired a 412-pace rail shot across the diagonal of the tennis court — exactly tying Hester's record — into a fleeing Frog Speedster. He climbed the Power Core to celebrate. The Power Core, which had been quiet for eight seasons, picked that moment to overload. Sundip did not survive the celebration. The League patched the overload that off-season.</p>
        <p>The Frog Speedster, in fairness, also died of the overload. The Frogs were given partial credit for posthumously denying Sundip his celebration. Hester's Rifle, which Sundip had been carrying, fell at the base of the Power Core and was scavenged in the post-match cleanup by a vault steward who has since requested unpaid leave.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Hall of Fallen Heroes, Sundip Memorial Wing, Seattle</div>
      </article>

      <article class="bio">
        <h3>Kor Wartooth</h3>
        <div class="meta">Wolf · Brawl · WLF · alive · Hallowtide Tag champion (LY 7, LY 8)</div>
        <p>Kor Wartooth is no relation to Crash Wartooth. The shared surname is solidarity — most Wolves who served on the LY 7 roster legally took on Crash's surname after he died, in the way some pack-Wolves take on the surname of a fallen pack-elder. The Commissioner's office accepted the paperwork without comment. There are now eleven living Wartooths and one dead one.</p>
        <p>Kor's career is unusual because most of it has happened in <em>melee-only</em> matches. In standard play he is a journeyman Brawler — competent, never spectacular, 14 kills across two seasons, no streaks longer than 4. In Hallowtide Tag he is an apex predator. He won the inaugural official Hallowtide Tag in LY 7 with one balloon remaining, the only living knight at the end of the seventy-eight-second match. He won it again in LY 8 with all three balloons intact.</p>
        <p>Asked by an LMG interviewer how he does it, Kor said: "I run faster when there are no guns." The interviewer asked if he was being literal. Kor said: "yes." The interviewer asked the team coach. The team coach said: "yes." The Wolves' kit-design office has, on three occasions, attempted to verify this claim with stopwatch trials in the off-season. The trials were inconclusive. The Wolves have stopped trialing.</p>
        <p>Kor will not enter the Coronation Cup. The crown's no-fire rule does not, on its face, prevent a melee Brawler from playing — but Kor has refused every entry the Wolves have offered. When asked, Kor says: "the crown is not for me." The Wolves do not press.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— "Profiles," LMG broadcast week of Hallowtide LY 8</div>
      </article>

      <article class="bio">
        <h3>Reka Pawington</h3>
        <div class="meta">Kit · Speed · KIT · alive · Pawington Cup record-holder (3 caps in a single match, LY 8)</div>
        <p>Reka Pawington is Mira Pawington's first cousin once removed. She entered the league in LY 8 as a rookie Speedster on the Kits' roster and announced, in her debut interview, that she had two career goals. The first was to win the Pawington Cup. The second was to <em>not</em> die in the same way Mira did, which she described as "with someone else's stuff in my hand and someone else's elbow in my back."</p>
        <p>She has not yet won the Cup. She has, however, set the single-match capture record in her debut Cup appearance: three captures in one match, against the Sharks, who finished with zero captures and several complaints about the carrier-speed rule. Reka's cap rate is widely attributed to her unusual tactical preference: instead of running directly back to base after a flag pickup, she runs in a long arc that takes her near the field's edges. The arc is approximately 30% longer than the direct route. Defenders consistently fail to anticipate it.</p>
        <p>Asked why she runs the arc, Reka has given different answers in different interviews. The most-cited is: "Mira always said the corners watch you. The middle doesn't care." League historians do not know what this means. Reka has refused to elaborate.</p>
        <p>The arc is now informally known as the Pawington Arc. Three other Speedsters across the league have attempted to copy it. None have replicated her cap rate. The Pawington Tradition's third clause — <em>announce the take</em> — is still observed by the Kits when Reka caps. The announcement is a single yip that echoes off Hardwood Hall's ceiling. The crowd has learned to wait for it.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— "Profiles," LMG broadcast week of Foundation Day LY 8</div>
      </article>

      <article class="bio">
        <h3>Talia Featherquill</h3>
        <div class="meta">Owl · journalist · The Wing Standard · ethics columnist · LY 6–present</div>
        <p>Talia Featherquill has never been a knight. She has never trained as one and has, on more than one occasion, declined invitations to the rookie-tryouts week sponsored by the Owls' civic affairs office. She is, instead, the longest-serving ethics columnist at The Wing Standard, the Seattle-based weekly that began publishing in League Year 6 and has not yet missed an issue.</p>
        <p>Her column appears in the Quarterly section, four times a year, and is widely read by knights, coaches, and the Commissioner's staff alike. She is famously hard to please. She has criticized the Personal Sandbag, the Power Core's overload latency, the Long Marketing Group's broadcast contract, and the Hallowtide Tag rule that allows the It knight to use a fallen prey's body as a tagging surface. She has also defended unpopular positions: that the Commissioner is right to refuse a sandbag-notification system, that the wider field is a structural improvement and not a betrayal of tradition, and that knights should be allowed to wear any color they want during the off-season.</p>
        <p>Her actual title at the paper is "staff columnist (ethics)." She also covers stadiums and obituaries when the regular reporters are unavailable. Her obituaries are widely considered the best in the League, in part because she refuses to write them in advance — she insists on hearing the news the same day everyone else does, and writes from the same first-shock state the readers will be in. She has written eleven knight obituaries since LY 6. She remembers all of them.</p>
        <p>Featherquill lives in Seattle near the Briarway Roost. She does not give interviews. She has, however, been the subject of one — an LMG broadcast profile in LY 8 that she declined to participate in, and which was therefore composed entirely of secondhand quotes from her colleagues, her editor, and one Otter Speedster who had once asked her for advice on accepting an endorsement deal. The Otter said Featherquill had told her: "if you have to ask me, you already know."</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Wing Standard staff page · author bio · LY 8 print edition</div>
      </article>

      <article class="bio">
        <h3>Pell Whittlestone</h3>
        <div class="meta">Badger · journeyman knight, then artisan · BDG (active LY 1–LY 4, retired) · forged the Coronation Cup crown</div>
        <p>Pell Whittlestone played four seasons as a Boise Brawling Badger Brawler before retiring at the end of LY 4 to take up metalwork full-time. Her career stats are modest: 18 kills across 4 seasons, no streaks longer than 5, never killed in a match (she retired ambulatory, with all her balloons intact, which is unusual). She is not in the Hall. She does not need to be.</p>
        <p>What she is known for is the crown. When the League formalized the Coronation Cup at the end of LY 4 and the Commissioner's Office solicited bids for a brass crown to be delivered to whichever team won the inaugural Cup, Whittlestone submitted a single page in response: a sketch of the crown she would make, a list of materials she would need, and a price quoted in lobster instead of bits. The Commissioner's Office — staffed at the time by an intern who did not know how unusual the lobster quote was — accepted the bid. The crown was forged at Whittlestone's workshop in Boise across six weeks.</p>
        <p>The crown weighs four pounds. It is brass, hand-hammered, with a rim engraved by Whittlestone in a script that the League's calligraphers have identified as Boise vernacular but cannot decode beyond the obvious team-emblem motifs. The League has asked Whittlestone, on three separate occasions, to translate the engraving. Whittlestone has, on three occasions, declined. "It is not for the League," she has said, in three slightly different versions. "It is for the head that wears it."</p>
        <p>The crown is reported missing the morning after every Coronation Cup. The crown is recovered, in some manner, before the next Cup. The crown's engravings have grown, over the years — there is a new sigil added each season, and Whittlestone is the only person known to have access to the workshop where the engravings happen. She receives the crown by mail, returns it by mail, and bills the League in lobster. The Foundation pays in lobster. The Brewer's Reach lobster vendor supplies it. The accounting is, by every reasonable measure, sealed.</p>
        <p>Whittlestone lives in Boise. She does not coach. She does not train. She will, however, accept an apprentice — there is a current apprentice, an unnamed Badger pup, whose first independent piece (a brass match-medal, presented at the LY 8 Wichita Open) was widely admired. The apprentice is not yet doing crowns.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Foundation Day program, LY 8 — "The Hands That Make the Crowns"</div>
      </article>

      <article class="bio">
        <h3>Wikrai Wartooth Jr.</h3>
        <div class="meta">Wolf · Speed · WLF · alive · son of Wikrai Wartooth Sr. (deceased LY 6)</div>
        <p>Wikrai Wartooth Jr. is the only living knight in the League whose father was also a knight in the League. Most knight bloodlines, by the third generation, have at least one living parent — usually a coach, sometimes a vendor. Wikrai Jr.'s father, Wikrai Sr., died on the field in LY 6, taken in the Boise Final by an opportune Power Cell. Wikrai Jr. was eleven years old. He decided, at the funeral, that he would become a Wolf. He decided, the same week, that he would not adopt the Wartooth surname through legal solidarity (which his cousins did the next year) but would inherit it patrilineally, as the original Wartooths had done. The Pack accepted the decision. The Pack rarely refuses a grieving cub.</p>
        <p>His career is mid-tier and steady: 22 kills across three seasons, longest streak 7, two scavenges. He is not the most spectacular Wolf on the WLF roster. He is, however, the most-petitioned Wolf on the roster — meaning that fans send him more letters than anyone else, mostly thanking him for something his father did. He answers all of them. The replies are short. The replies are, by all accounts, kind.</p>
        <p>His most consequential off-field act was the Hallowtide petition. After the LY 6 informal pack-bonding ritual went well, the WLF coaching staff filed paperwork to make the ritual a formal League event. The paperwork went nowhere for two years. In LY 7, Wikrai Jr. — then 17, in his rookie season — refiled the petition under his own name, with one new sentence: <em>"My father did not get to play this. The next generation should."</em> The Commissioner approved the formalization within a week. Hallowtide Tag has been an official League event since LY 7. Kor Wartooth has won it twice. Wikrai Jr. has not yet won it. He has, in both years, made the bracket's quarterfinals and lost to Kor in matched-Wolf finals. He is at peace with this. The Pack notices that he is at peace with this. The fanbase loves him for it.</p>
        <p>He does not bow after kills. The Pack ritual is observed by older Wolves; Wikrai Jr. was raised on it and considers it sacred enough that he will not perform it casually. He is reported to have said, in a late-night LMG interview that the producer cut for length: "the bow is for fathers. I don't have one."</p>
        <div class="signoff">— LMG late-broadcast interview, LY 8 Hallowtide eve · uncut transcript filed at WLF press office</div>
      </article>

      <article class="bio">
        <h3>Old Bristlebeard the Third</h3>
        <div class="meta">Beaver · Tank · BVR · alive · current League Year 8, third Bristlebeard, first to survive a season</div>
        <p>The Beavers have had three Bristlebeards. Old Bristlebeard the First died defending the inaugural championship final in LY 1. Old Bristlebeard the Second walked into Otter fire on the opening kickoff of LY 5 and was felled before the round's first minute. The Third, currently active, has survived eight matches. This is unprecedented. The Beavers are nervous about it.</p>
        <p>Bristlebeard the Third — Bjorn, in private — is twenty-two years old, the youngest Tank in League history at his debut, and an outlier in nearly every Beaver tradition. He does not, as the First and Second did, charge straight at the first enemy he sees. He does not, as Beaver Tanks have for eight seasons, lead the field. He plays a defensive position, holds the back line, and lets his Brawlers commit first. The fanbase is split. The older fanbase wants him to charge. The younger fanbase wants him to live.</p>
        <p>His coach, when asked about the unconventional play style, has said: "Bjorn is the first Bristlebeard who watched the previous Bristlebeards die. We do not coach him out of that." The coach was photographed saying this. The photo is in the Boston press office. Bjorn does not comment on it.</p>
        <p>He carries Old Bristlebeard the First's Riot Shield. The Shield has had seven carriers across six seasons. Bjorn is the seventh. He acquired the Shield in LY 8 R3 — it was scavenged from Wikrai Wartooth Sr.'s carrier in a gear exchange that the LMG broadcast missed and the next morning's papers had to reconstruct from witness reports. The Shield does not, by Bjorn's account, weigh more than any other Riot Shield. The Shield does, however, attract attention. Crowds cheer it. Photographers track it. The Foundation has made an offer to vault it; Bjorn declined the offer. "It's not done yet," he said, declining.</p>
        <p>If Bjorn survives this season — and the betting markets, after eight matches, currently put the odds at 3-to-1 favor of survival — he will be the first Bristlebeard in League history to play a second season. He will also, statistically, become the longest-serving Beaver Tank ever. The Beavers, who are still nervous, are also quietly proud.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— "The Survivor's Burden," Wing Standard feature, LY 8 mid-season</div>
      </article>

      <article class="bio">
        <h3>Jossi Treadwell</h3>
        <div class="meta">Owl · LMG production director · Reno academy head instructor · never played a match</div>
        <p>Jossi Treadwell is, by any reasonable accounting, the most important person in the League's broadcast apparatus. She is also, by her own consistent insistence, the least photographed. The Wing Standard ran a feature on the LMG production team in LY 7; Treadwell agreed to be interviewed but declined to be photographed. The article ran with a photograph of her empty operator's chair at Hardwood Hall, the camera dolly behind it visible, the chair turned to face the action. It is, by the photographer's account, the photograph she preferred.</p>
        <p>She was not a knight. This fact gets repeated in every short reference to her in League records — <em>the LMG production director, an Owl, who has never been a knight</em> — because it is rare. Most of the League's adjacent professionals (referees, statisticians, broadcast crew) come from washed-out or retired playing careers. Treadwell never had a playing career. She apprenticed in the pre-Collapse Boston public access studio — a five-room cinder block building with one functional camera and a sign on the door that read NO FOOTBALL FOOTAGE WITHOUT WRITTEN CONSENT, a sign Treadwell has reportedly kept and now hangs in her Reno office — when the Collapse hit. She was sixteen. The studio's last director, an older Owl whose name has not survived in League records, taught her the dolly rig, the framing protocols, the timing-call protocols, and the ethics of broadcast (the older Owl's stated rule, which Treadwell has quoted in every academy lecture she gives: <em>"You do not own the moment. You frame it. The audience owns it. If you frame it wrong, you have stolen it from them."</em>) over the course of three years. The studio closed when the older Owl died. Treadwell took the camera dolly with her.</p>
        <p>She arrived at Brewer's Reach in LY 1, week one, with the dolly in the back of a salvaged truck and a written request to be allowed to film the inaugural match. Old Bristlebeard the First, then the Beavers' coach, granted permission on the condition that she not photograph any individual knight without written permission from the knight first. Treadwell agreed in writing. The agreement, by League records, has been honored every match since. Knights who have asked, in subsequent seasons, whether they were filmed during a particular play have always received an honest answer; knights who have not asked have not been filmed individually, only in wide shot. The League has, on three occasions, asked Treadwell to deviate from this rule for legal-evidence purposes (the LY 4 Phoenix incident, the LY 6 Coalbroke truck-stop investigation, and an unspecified third matter the records have sealed). She has declined all three times. The League has, all three times, accepted her decline.</p>
        <p>She moved the LMG production booth to Hardwood Hall in LY 6 when the dynamic-camera prototype was being tested; she has lived in Kansas since. Her Reno academy is run remotely. She returns to Reno four times a year for the Cup-cycle finals and the academy's intake week. Her assistant production directors — there are three of them now, all from the Reno academy, all Owls — handle the regular-season broadcasts she does not personally cover. She still operates the camera personally for every Coronation Cup, Pawington Cup, and Hallowtide Tag final.</p>
        <p>Featherquill, who has known Treadwell since LY 2, has profiled her exactly once and closed the profile with the only sentence Treadwell has ever publicly co-signed: <em>"Treadwell will not be remembered as a great owl. Treadwell will be remembered as the owl who watched."</em> Treadwell, reading this, is reported to have nodded once, said "that's correct," and gone back to looking at the field.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Wing Standard, "The Owl in the Booth" — Featherquill, LY 7 mid-season feature</div>
      </article>

      <article class="bio">
        <h3>Brask Ironclaw III</h3>
        <div class="meta">Fox · Tank · FOX · deceased LY 4 · son of Brask Sr. (head coach FOX, retired)</div>
        <p>Brask Ironclaw III was the third generation of his family to play Tank for the Phoenix Foxes. His grandfather served on the founding-era roster of LY 1 in a supporting role and never started a match; his father, Brask Sr., started fourteen matches across LY 2 and LY 3 before transitioning to coaching when Brask III made the active roster. The handoff was, by every account from the Foxes' bench, deliberate. Brask III was eighteen years old when he debuted. His father stepped behind the boards within the same week.</p>
        <p>His career lasted six and a half matches. He was the second-youngest Tank in League history at debut (the youngest is now Bjorn Bristlebeard, who has the additional distinction of having survived his rookie season). Brask III's stats at his death were not remarkable: 9 kills, longest streak 4, average lives remaining at round-end 1.7. The numbers do not reflect what he was. What he was, by general agreement, was a Tank who would walk into a corridor he knew was a kill zone if his Healer was on the wrong side of it.</p>
        <p>He died at home, at the Phoenix Sandstone Bowl, in a R5 against the Wichita Wolves. The match was a Coronation Cup qualifier. The death was a Crash Wartooth pin — three Wolves on Brask, two of them carrying Plasma Maces, no clear shot for Brask's own teammates. He held the corridor for eleven seconds. His Healer reached him at the eight-second mark and was killed by the second Mace. Brask III's last balloon popped at the eleventh second. The Wolves did not, by tradition or by witness account, bow.</p>
        <p>His father coached the entire match from the boards. Brask Sr. did not call for a substitution. Brask Sr. did not call for retreat. After the round, Brask Sr. stood up, walked around the boards, and sat next to his son's body on the field for the seven minutes the field crew needed to clear it. He has not commented publicly on those seven minutes. The Foxes' assistant coach, who had to step in to manage the rest of the bracket, said only: "He was the head coach. He was also the father. He sat with his son. There was nothing else to coach in those seven minutes."</p>
        <p>Brask Sr. retired from coaching at the end of the LY 4 season. The Foxes hired the assistant. Brask Sr. is, as of the current League Year, working as a metalsmith in Phoenix's old industrial quarter. He has not attended a Foxes match since his son's funeral. He has, however, been seen at Olympia matches when the Foxes were in the bracket — not as a coach, not in colors, just as a fan in the back rows. He pays for his ticket. The lobster vendor at Brewer's Reach has, at least once, been seen at a Phoenix–Olympia road game, sharing his row, saying nothing.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Phoenix municipal archives, "Foxes Roster Histories Vol. III," compiled LY 6</div>
      </article>

      <article class="bio">
        <h3>The Lobster Vendor of Brewer's Reach</h3>
        <div class="meta">Crustacean · vendor · Brewer's Reach concession booth · uncertain age · possibly canonical</div>
        <p>The lobster vendor of Brewer's Reach is a real person. This is the most that can be said with certainty. Beyond it, the record is contradictory in almost every direction — and the contradictions are themselves part of the canon, repeated faithfully and gravely across eight seasons of attempted profiles.</p>
        <p>What is agreed: she runs a single concession booth on the south concourse of the Brewer's Reach Pitch in Boston. She has run it since the inaugural match of LY 1, every match the Pitch has hosted, including the bracket-bye matches when no Beavers were playing. She sells four things — lobster bisque, lobster rolls, lobster claws (steamed), and a small cup of clarified butter she calls "the dip." She sells nothing else. She has, on three documented occasions, refused to sell food to specific patrons; in two of those cases the patron was a Wolf, and in the third the patron was a former Beavers Tank who had publicly criticized her bisque the prior season. The record does not say whether she relented.</p>
        <p>What is contested: her age, her species (a clade of lobster-adjacent crustaceans is documented in the early-League rosters but the vendor herself has never been registered as a player or staff member of any formal League body), her residence, and the precise mechanism by which she travels to road games. She has been documented at Phoenix in LY 4, sitting silently with Brask Ironclaw Sr. in the back rows of the away stand. She has been documented at Reno, weeping in the concession area on the night of Sundip Hootington Jr.'s death — though the dispatch reporter took care to clarify that the Reno vendor was a different person, suggesting either the Brewer's Reach vendor was also there, or the dispatch reporter was being cautious about a thing he could not explain. She has been documented at Helena Glacier Rink on the night of Hester Quillstrike's death. The pattern, by every reading of the documents, is that she appears for the deaths.</p>
        <p>What is folklore: she is older than the League. She was the first vendor at the first match because she was at the site before the Pitch was built, in a small wooden cart she pulled herself. She does not age. She has never been photographed clearly enough for any reporter to confirm the same vendor across two adjacent seasons, but the vendor at the booth always says, when asked, "I have been here." She bills the Foundation in lobster, of course. The Foundation pays in lobster. The accounting, as Whittlestone's accounting does, makes its own internal sense.</p>
        <p>What is true: the bisque is excellent. Reporters who have profiled her over the eight seasons of the League's existence universally agree that the bisque is excellent. The bisque has been the same recipe since LY 1, by the vendor's account; she has, on one occasion, agreed to share the recipe with a culinary journalist from Olympia who ran the recipe in a small culinary quarterly. The recipe, when followed precisely, does not produce the bisque. The journalist tested it three times. The journalist reported, in a follow-up column, that "there is something in the bisque that is not in the recipe, and the vendor has not lied — the recipe is hers — but it is not all of it." The vendor, when asked about the column, said: "I have been here." She did not elaborate.</p>
        <p>An anonymous BVR season-pass holder, in a notebook entry covering the LY 4 funeral of Brask Ironclaw III, recorded the following: "The lobster vendor was at the funeral. I do not know how she got to Phoenix." It is the most honest sentence anyone has written about her.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Wing Standard cultural feature, "Standing Witness," LY 7 — author declined attribution</div>
      </article>

      <article class="bio">
        <h3>Ojee Stripeshoulder</h3>
        <div class="meta">Tiger matriarch · Bayou Burning Tigers · signatory of the Four-Team Expansion application · LY 8 closeout</div>
        <p>Ojee Stripeshoulder did not, by any account that has reached the League, set out to be the matriarch of the Bayou Tigers. She set out to be a respected Striker, broke her left hind leg in a salvage-warehouse accident at age twenty-six, lost the playing speed that had been her livelihood, and decided — three months into a forced rehabilitation she has never described publicly — to spend the rest of her career teaching tigers half her age how to stalk an opening. The Bayou roster, on the day she became matriarch, had four registered players. The roster on the day she signed the joint Expansion application had thirty-four. Twenty-eight of them learned to play under her.</p>
        <p>Her stripe-painting practice — she paints every helmet on the active roster before every match, by hand, with a pigment she mixes herself from cypress-bark resin and a closely-guarded second ingredient — is widely believed by the team to be ceremonial. It is not entirely. The pigment is also a slow-curing antifungal. Bayou helmets, in the delta humidity, would otherwise mold within four matches. The ceremony is real <em>and</em> the helmet is preserved. Stripeshoulder, when asked whether the ceremony or the antifungal was the more important purpose, said: <em>"I do not separate things that have arrived together."</em> She has not elaborated on this answer in any subsequent interview, and reporters have stopped asking.</p>
        <p>Her opening line in the joint Expansion application — <em>"We have waited"</em> — was, by the Office of the Commissioner's records, written first by her, then quietly co-signed by the other three signatories before the document was sent. Whitepaw is reported to have read it twice, said "yes, that is correct," and signed. Yellowhide is reported to have grunted approval, then offered to add a sentence that was politely declined by the other three. Quorrik is reported to have asked whether the Tigers were sure about the word "waited" — Ravens, by tradition, do not wait for invitations — and Stripeshoulder is reported to have said, after a long pause: <em>"You waited too. You did not call it that."</em> Quorrik signed without further comment.</p>
        <p>Stripeshoulder will not, by her own statement, play in any LY 9 match. She is an active matriarch, not an active knight. She will be in the stands. She will paint the helmets. She will, her assistant coach has confirmed, not coach from the bench during matches — the Tigers run a deliberately non-shouting bench, in keeping with their stalking style — but she will be visible to her knights from the stands at all times. Her chair, in every Bayou Cypress-Hollow venue, is in the same row as the field marshal's. She watches.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Wing Standard, "The Matriarch of the Cypress Hollow" · LY 8 R12, by Talia Featherquill</div>
      </article>

      <article class="bio">
        <h3>Tamzen Whitepaw</h3>
        <div class="meta">Polar Bear elder · Anchorage Avalanche Polar Bears · signatory of the Four-Team Expansion application · LY 8 closeout</div>
        <p>Tamzen Whitepaw walked from Anchorage to Brewer's Reach. So did the rest of the Polar Bears' delegation, but Whitepaw, by every account, walked first — at the front of the column, breaking trail through the late-spring snow, for the first eight days of what became a thirty-two-day journey. The other elders rotated the lead through the remaining twenty-four days. Whitepaw did not. She walked the entire trail at the front. When asked, on arrival, whether she was tired, she answered: <em>"I am the same amount of tired I was when we left. The walk is what tired looks like."</em></p>
        <p>She is, by the Anchorage settlement's records, ninety-one years old. The Polar Bears do not, as a species, retire from civic responsibility — Whitepaw has been the chief elder of the Anchorage settlement since LY 2, when the previous chief elder died of old age at one hundred and four — and she has stated, in writing, that she will continue to lead the team's elder council "for as long as the council finds my judgment useful." The council has not held a vote on this matter. The Polar Bears do not, by tradition, take votes on whether their elders should still be elders.</p>
        <p>Her armor, custom-forged in Anchorage by the settlement's lead smith Bjorgun Whitemane, is widely believed to be the heaviest functional armor in the League. Whitemane will not disclose its weight on the record but has, off the record, told visiting reporters that "you would not be able to lift it. You would not be able to lift the helmet alone. The helmet alone is not the heaviest part. The helmet is the lightest part." Whitepaw wears it in matches. She moves slowly. She does not move quickly even when the round demands it. The Polar Bear style, by Whitepaw's own description, is: <em>"slow the storm. outlast it."</em> Polar Bear Tanks have, in the team's pre-League barnstorming exhibitions, soaked thirty consecutive close-range hits without falling. Stripeshoulder, after attending one of these exhibitions, sent Whitepaw a brief note: <em>"We will not stalk you. We do not have time."</em> Whitepaw kept the note.</p>
        <p>She has, by the application's record, exactly one stated worry about the LY 9 season: that the bracket draw will pull the Polar Bears in their first season more often than the random distribution should. She mentioned this, conversationally, to Whiskerstone after the application was ratified. Whiskerstone is reported to have said: <em>"The draw will be the draw. If you are drawn often, you are drawn often. We will not adjust."</em> Whitepaw nodded. She has not raised the subject again.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Wing Standard, "The Bear Who Walked First" · LY 8 R13, by Talia Featherquill</div>
      </article>

      <article class="bio">
        <h3>Yellowhide of the Denver Herd</h3>
        <div class="meta">Bison elder · Denver Driving Bison · ceremonial lead, attends every home match in full harness · LY 8 closeout</div>
        <p>Yellowhide is twenty-two years old, which is old for a bison and is not, by Denver custom, the kind of age a fighting elder reaches. He fought competitively from age four to age fourteen — ten seasons of plains-circuit matches, no League affiliation, no records the Float Foundation can verify, only the testimony of Denver knights who saw him fight and who all describe his style identically: <em>he stood where he stood, and the other team adjusted around him, and then we won</em>. He stopped fighting after a horn fracture in his eleventh year that did not heal cleanly. He has, since, served as the herd's lead elder.</p>
        <p>His ceremonial harness — leather, worked with copper and stained with Denver red dust — was made for him by the herd's saddler in LY 2 and has been resized twice as he has aged. He wears it to every Bison home match. He stands at the south end of the Highland-Plains pitch in full harness for the duration of every match the Bison play in Denver. He does not coach. He does not heckle. He stands. The Bison knights, by the herd's coaching staff's account, do not need him to coach — they need him to stand. The Bison's herd-rotation tactical style requires every knight to know, at any moment, where the herd's center of gravity is. Yellowhide is, by tradition, that gravity. The knights orient on him without thinking about it. The opposing teams, by the third match against the Bison in the Bison's pre-League exhibitions, learned to orient on him too.</p>
        <p>He signed the Four-Team Expansion application with a hoof-print in red dust, the way Denver elders sign formal documents. The print is on file at the Office of the Commissioner. The Office's records-keeper, an elderly otter named Kirrin, has reportedly photographed it for the archive and added a single annotated note in his hand: <em>"This is a signature. We have accepted other signatures that meant less."</em></p>
        <p>Yellowhide, asked whether he intended to attend Bison away matches as well in LY 9, said: <em>"I attend the matches the herd plays at home. I am the herd. The herd cannot leave the home. The herd that travels is a delegation. They will be fine."</em> Whiskerstone, on hearing this, is reported to have agreed without comment.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Float Foundation Atlas Committee, "On Yellowhide" · LY 8 closeout, internal note circulated to coaching staff</div>
      </article>

      <article class="bio">
        <h3>Quorrik</h3>
        <div class="meta">Raven captain · Pittsburgh Plundering Ravens · signatory of the Four-Team Expansion application · LY 8 closeout</div>
        <p>Quorrik — recorded only by single name in League documents because Ravens, by tradition, only take their parent's name after their first kill in defense of the murder, and Quorrik has not yet had occasion — is twenty-six years old, the youngest of the four Expansion signatories, and the only one who is also, by team designation, an active knight. Quorrik's roster position is Sniper. The Ravens' Sniper class is the most-decorated archery class in the League's pre-League barnstorming records. Quorrik, by their own coach's account, is the second-best Sniper in the Ravens' active roster. The best Sniper, an older Raven named Skell, has declined to comment on Quorrik's ranking. Skell has, however, declined to take the captaincy when offered, on the grounds that "Quorrik should hold it. Quorrik will hold it longer than I will need to."</p>
        <p>Quorrik writes left-handed. This matters because the Ravens' Snipers, by long custom, all draw the bowstring with the right hand and the arrow-rest is set up accordingly on every Ravens-issue Sniper bow. Quorrik's bow, custom-made by a Pittsburgh fletcher named Erris who has served the murder for thirty years, is a left-handed mirror of the standard Ravens bow with a mirrored arrow-rest. Erris, when asked whether the bow was harder to make, said: <em>"It was the same difficulty. The bow does not have an opinion. The fletcher had to remember to turn the workshop the other way around."</em></p>
        <p>The Ravens, as a team, do not bury their dead. They cremate, the way they have always cremated, in the foundry-fires of the Pittsburgh ruins. Quorrik will, as captain, lead the cremation ceremony for any Raven knight who falls during the LY 9 season. They have stated, in writing, that they are prepared for this. They have stated, also in writing, that they hope not to perform it more than once.</p>
        <p>Quorrik attended the Caucus's ratification meeting in Boston. They flew. The flight from Pittsburgh to Boston took six days with three rest stops. Quorrik arrived two hours before the Caucus opened its session. They did not, by Whiskerstone's record, sleep before speaking. When asked, after the ratification, whether they were tired, they answered: <em>"I will be tired in Pittsburgh. Not here."</em> They flew home. The Ravens' fletcher Erris is reported to have met them at the perch with a fresh-strung bow, said nothing, and walked them home.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Wing Standard, "The Captain Who Flies" · LY 8 R13, by Talia Featherquill</div>
      </article>

      <article class="bio">
        <h3>Tessik Snowstep</h3>
        <div class="meta">Lynx coach · Yukon Hunting Lynx · sole delegate of the LY 9 Continental Expansion · arrived alone</div>
        <p>Tessik Snowstep is the only person in League records to have personally hand-carried five active knights' rosters and credentials across the entire continent without bringing any of those knights with them. Tessik did this because the Lynx considered it inappropriate for the knights to travel together. The Lynx, by tradition, do not travel in groups larger than two; the knights would have come, but they would have come in five separate parties along five separate routes, and would have arrived at five different times across approximately a six-week window. The Office of the Commissioner did not, in its initial reply to the Yukon Boreal Council, fully understand this. The Office of the Commissioner suggested the Lynx send a delegation. Tessik replied, in a single sentence: <em>"I am the delegation. The knights will play the matches."</em></p>
        <p>Tessik is, by Yukon Council records, fifty-three years old, retired from active competition since LY 4 (a knee injury sustained on a hunt that the Council records do not specify the species of), and has been the senior coach of the Lynx team since the team's formation in LY 7. Tessik holds the rare distinction of being one of the only Lynx in known living memory to be photographed: there are exactly three photographs of Tessik in the League's records, all three taken at the LY 9 Caucus's ratification meeting, all three taken with Tessik's prior written consent, all three taken from approximately the same angle. Tessik does not consider this a personal preference. Tessik considers it a professional accommodation. <em>"The League's audience needs to be able to recognize the coaches,"</em> Tessik said when asked. <em>"I will be in five photographs over the course of my coaching career. We have agreed in advance which five. I have already used three."</em></p>
        <p>Tessik's coaching style does not, by their own description, involve a great deal of speaking to the players. The Lynx training program is constructed around solitary preparation: each knight is assigned a coach for their position class (the team has five coaches, one per position) and trains alone with that coach for the bulk of the season. Tessik's role as senior coach is to <em>review</em> the training, not to <em>direct</em> it. They write notes. The notes are short. The longest note, on file in the Yukon Council records, is forty-one words long; it concerns a Sniper named Kell who, by Tessik's observation, was holding their breath wrong before each shot. Kell adjusted. The note has not been needed again.</p>
        <p>Tessik, asked at the founding bell what their hopes were for the LY 9 season, answered: <em>"I hope my knights play well. I hope they come home. I hope I can return to the Yukon by mid-season — I have been away for some time, and I am tired of being among so many people."</em> The interviewer pointed out that the Lynx's first scheduled match was eight weeks out and Tessik would, presumably, have to remain in Boston until at least then. Tessik said: <em>"Yes. I will be very tired by then."</em></p>
        <div class="signoff">— Yukon Boreal Council, "Coach Profile: Tessik Snowstep" · LY 9 founding bell</div>
      </article>

      <article class="bio">
        <h3>Bertaud Antlerwood</h3>
        <div class="meta">Moose patriarch · Quebec Crashing Moose · senior delegate · 9'2" at the shoulder, antler span 6'4"</div>
        <p>Bertaud Antlerwood is, by physical measurement, the largest knight in the League. He stands nine feet two inches at the shoulder. His antler span is six feet four inches at full racks (the antlers shed and regrow seasonally; the LY 9 spring rack is, by the Quebec roster's measurement, the largest he has ever grown). Bertaud is also, by his own assessment, the slowest knight in the League. He moves, he says, "at the speed I move. The other knights have agreed not to ask me to move faster, because they have, on multiple occasions, tried to ask, and have not enjoyed the experience."</p>
        <p>The Moose, as a species, are not native French speakers — French was the prior species' language, and the Moose simply continued to use it after the prior species was gone, in the way the Pittsburgh ravens continued to use the foundry. Bertaud, however, speaks a dialect that has descended further than most: a creole of pre-Collapse French and an animal-tongue the Moose call, simply, <em>la langue</em>. The LMG broadcast booth has, for the LY 9 season, hired a dedicated translator — an Owl named Pellic Snowcrest, formerly the Geography Caucus, now part-time interpreter — to render Bertaud's match-time observations into League Common. Snowcrest's working notes describe Bertaud's speech as <em>"slower than League Common, more poetic than League Common, and, by my preliminary count, requiring approximately three League-Common sentences to render each Moose sentence accurately. I will need a larger notebook."</em></p>
        <p>Bertaud's antler rack is, under League rules, an attached weapon. The Moose argued for this designation in their original LY 5 application; the Caucus had, at the time, ruled that the rack was not an attached weapon because the rack was not <em>removable</em>. The Moose's revised LY 9 application addressed this point directly: <em>"The antler is not removable. This is correct. The antler is also lethal. We are confident the audience would prefer to know the antler counts as a weapon, rather than be surprised by the antler when the Moose reaches a Brawler at melee range. We are flexible on the wording. We are not flexible on the audience knowing."</em> The Caucus, on a 12-3 vote, classified the antler as an attached weapon for the LY 9 season forward. The Moose did not comment.</p>
        <p>Bertaud, asked at the founding bell whether he was looking forward to playing in front of a non-Quebec audience, answered (through Snowcrest's translation): <em>"The audience is the audience. I have always played in front of an audience. The audience in Quebec speaks Moose. The audience in Boston speaks League Common. The audience in Anchorage will, I am told, speak Polar Bear, which I am told sounds approximately like the wind makes when it is cold and tired. I will be all right. The audience will be all right. We will all be all right."</em></p>
        <div class="signoff">— LMG Broadcast Brief, "Translator's Notes" · Snowcrest, supervising · LY 9 founding bell</div>
      </article>

      <article class="bio">
        <h3>Yara of the Sonora pack</h3>
        <div class="meta">Coyote captain · Sonora Sly Coyotes · pack leader since LY 5</div>
        <p>Yara of the Sonora pack does not, by Coyote tradition, have a surname. The Coyotes do not take surnames. Coyotes identify themselves by their pack — Yara of the Sonora pack is one Yara; there is also (by Sonora roster records) a Yara of the Mojave pack who is unrelated, a Yara of the Apache pack who is also unrelated, and at least two further Yaras whose packs are not currently in League records. The disambiguation system is functional within the Coyote community. The League's broadcast booth has, by Treadwell's recommendation, agreed to refer to Yara on first reference as "Yara of the Sonora pack" and on subsequent references simply as "Yara," with the understanding that any other Yaras who later enter League records will receive equivalent first-reference framings.</p>
        <p>Yara has been the Sonora pack's leader since LY 5. The transition from previous leader (a Coyote whose name does not appear in League records but whom the Sonora pack remembers as <em>"the one before Yara"</em>) was, by all accounts, peaceful. The previous leader walked into the desert one evening and did not return. Yara walked out the next morning to find the previous leader's tracks, followed them for half a day, found a single coyote-print pointing west, and turned around. The pack accepted Yara's leadership on her return. The previous leader has not been confirmed missing or dead by any Coyote authority — the Sonora pack considers the previous leader to be <em>"out there, somewhere, doing what they are doing"</em> — but the previous leader has also not, by League records, been seen since.</p>
        <p>Yara's tactical signature, the so-called <em>desert pattern</em>, was first documented in pre-League exhibition matches in LY 6 and has been the Sonora pack's primary engagement style since. The pattern, as Featherquill described it: <em>"A coyote will appear to retreat, then appear to retreat again, then appear to be alone, then six coyotes will engage from positions the opposing team had thought were already cleared."</em> The pattern requires the entire pack to commit to misdirection in the same beat — and the same precise timing. The Sonora pack has, by their training records, drilled the pattern eleven thousand times. They do not, by Yara's own account, ever expect to drill it ten thousand more.</p>
        <p>Yara, asked at the founding bell whether she was confident the desert pattern would translate to non-desert venues, answered: <em>"The pattern is not about the desert. The pattern is about <em>asking the desert</em>. The desert is what we have been listening to. We can listen anywhere. The Sonora is the desert that taught us how to listen. The other venues are other deserts that have not yet been told they are deserts. We will tell them."</em> Featherquill's marginal note on the interview transcript: <em>"I think she means it."</em></p>
        <div class="signoff">— Wing Standard, "Asking the Desert" — Featherquill, LY 9 founding bell preview feature</div>
      </article>

      <article class="bio">
        <h3>Cintzli of the Yucatan canopy</h3>
        <div class="meta">Quetzal captain · Yucatan Vibrant Quetzals · sniper · plumage measurement on file with the Caucus</div>
        <p>Cintzli of the Yucatan canopy is the smallest active knight in the League by mass. She is also, by her own coach's measurement, the most distinctively colored: her crest is brilliant emerald, her breast is deep crimson, her wing-coverts are a shifted iridescent green that the Caucus's official plumage record describes (with apparent reluctance) as <em>"approximately the color of a particular kind of moss reflected through a particular kind of water; we have not been able to fix this color in pigment"</em>. The Quetzals, as a species, do not consider their coloration unusual. The other species in the League consider it remarkable. The Foxes' captain, on first seeing Cintzli at a pre-season inter-team meet, is reported to have said: <em>"I have just learned that we have been playing the wrong sport this whole time. The sport should be the kind that this Quetzal wins."</em></p>
        <p>Cintzli is the Yucatan team's sniper. Her preferred shooting position is in the rafters of the Yucatan Jungle-Canopy venue, in a nest she has personally maintained since the team's formation. The nest is not, by League records, registered as terrain. The Caucus has discussed registering it. The Caucus has, on each occasion, decided not to register it, on the grounds that "the rafters were here before the team was; the nest was here before the team was; the Quetzal was, by her own account, here before the rafters were; we cannot retrofit the venue around the player's nest, but we are also not in a position to ask the player to relocate her nest."</p>
        <p>Cintzli speaks four languages: Quetzal native, League Common, what the Yucatan refers to as <em>"the trees' language"</em> (which is, by Cintzli's own description, <em>"a way of asking the trees to keep being trees while we sit in them"</em>), and a working pre-Collapse Spanish she learned from the runes around the Yucatan basin. She has been, on three occasions, asked to translate Spanish runes for visiting League archivists. She has, on each occasion, said yes. On each of those occasions she has charged a fee of one fresh fruit. She has been paid in fruit she selected each time. The Spanish translations she has provided are reported to be excellent.</p>
        <p>Cintzli, asked at the founding bell whether she was concerned the LY 9 schedule would put her in venues without rafters, answered: <em>"Every venue has a high point. Every high point can be a nest if I bring my own moss. I have brought my own moss. The League will see what I do with it."</em></p>
        <div class="signoff">— Caucus Plumage Records, "Cintzli of the Yucatan canopy" · LY 9 founding bell</div>
      </article>
    </div>

    <!-- ===== DIARIES ===== -->
