      <article class="log">
        <h3>Match Log: Coronation Cup, LY 8 — RCC vs BVR (Reno, Open Court)</h3>
        <div class="meta">Official sportscaster transcript · 11:42 game time · partial · disputed by all parties</div>
        <p>00:04 — Crown materializes at center of court. Both teams sprint for it.</p>
        <p>00:09 — Mok Mok (RCC Tank) reaches the crown first. Crown is on Mok Mok's head. Mok Mok cannot fire. Mok Mok runs.</p>
        <p>01:23 — Score 11-0 in favor of RCC. The Beavers have not yet reached the crown carrier. The Beavers' Speedster (a rookie named Stan Whittlebark) attempts to cut Mok Mok off near the field's eastern fence. Mok Mok turns. Stan does not turn. Stan runs into the fence.</p>
        <p>02:51 — Score 28-0. The Beavers have closed within shooting range. Mok Mok takes a Plasma Mace to the back from a Beaver Brawler. Crown drops at Mok Mok's position.</p>
        <p>02:52 — Heeya Striperaccoon (RCC Brawler) picks up the crown before any Beaver reaches it. Heeya cannot fire. Heeya runs.</p>
        <p>03:14 — Crown is on Heeya. Score 31-0. The Beavers' Sniper (Brand Tooth) lines up a shot. The shot connects. Heeya falls.</p>
        <p>03:15 — Lurkov Bandit (RCC Speed) picks up the crown. Lurkov cannot fire. Lurkov runs.</p>
        <p>03:42 — Brand Tooth lines up a second shot on Lurkov. The shot misses by, the LMG estimates, four paces. The Beavers' bench complains the shot was clean. The League's review office has the footage. The footage is, the League says, "inconclusive."</p>
        <p>04:18 — Score 41-0. Crown is on Lurkov, who is now running in a tight circle near the RCC bench because the Beavers have set up a perimeter. The crown's points-per-second tick continues regardless of how much actual ground the carrier covers. Coaches across the league have flagged this as a balance issue. The League has, formally, no opinion.</p>
        <p>05:09 — Score 50-0. Match over. RCC wins the Coronation Cup for the sixth time.</p>
        <p>05:10 — Commissioner takes possession of the crown for the standard post-match inspection. The inspection takes thirty seconds. The crown is returned to the RCC dugout intact and unmodified.</p>
        <p>05:40 — RCC parade lap with the crown around the Reno Open Court perimeter.</p>
        <p>NEXT MORNING — The crown is reported "missing for inspection." The League's Atlas Committee opens a formal review.</p>
        <p>THREE WEEKS LATER — The crown is located. It is in a Reno pawn shop, three blocks from the Coliseum, with a price tag of 240 bits. The pawn shop owner is a Raccoon. The pawn shop owner says she "found it." The crown is recovered. The crown is engraved (this engraving is also disputed). The Cup is, formally, awarded to RCC for LY 8. The pawn shop owner is not arrested. The pawn shop owner is not <em>not</em> arrested. The pawn shop owner is given a small commendation by the Reno Cultural Office.</p>
        <p>The crown is in the League vault now. The vault is, technically, in Boston. The vault is, technically, locked. The vault is, technically, supervised by an LMG steward whose pocket is, technically, his own.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— LMG match summary · LY 8 Coronation Cup · "the most predictable upset in League history"</div>
      </article>

      <article class="log">
        <h3>Match Log: Season 8 R8, OWL vs FRG (Reno Open Court) — the 412-pace match</h3>
        <div class="meta">LMG broadcast transcript · 17:02 game time · uncut</div>
        <p>16:58 — End of round 4. OWL leads 2-1. Frogs take the next round and force a deciding fifth. Sundip Hootington Jr. (OWL Sniper) is the only OWL knight at full HP. He is carrying Hester Quillstrike's Railgun Rifle, which has been on his back since LY 8 R3.</p>
        <p>17:00 — Round 5 begins. Frogs adopt a four-Speed flank formation; OWL holds center. The Power Core, which has been quiet for eight seasons, is showing its standard pre-spawn glow. Coach Bristlewren of OWL has just been visible on the sideline cam saying "stay off the core" — he has been saying this every match for two years and the camera operator has stopped considering it newsworthy.</p>
        <p>17:01:15 — A Frog Speedster breaks left along the court's diagonal. Sundip tracks them. He is two hundred and forty units away, well within his railgun's effective range, and waiting for the line. The Speedster is moving fast.</p>
        <p>17:01:23 — Sundip fires. The shot is read live by the LMG kinetics tracker as having traveled four hundred and twelve paces along the diagonal of the Reno court. The Speedster's head balloon pops. The Speedster's body balloon pops a frame later from inertia. Two-balloon kill. Sundip has, by the kinetics tracker's official measurement, exactly tied Hester Quillstrike's longest-shot record. The crowd does not yet know this. The kinetics tracker tells the LMG broadcast booth, which tells the announcer.</p>
        <p>17:01:25 — Sundip's stat panel updates: the 412-pace co-record is now formally his. The League's Records Committee will, in the morning, confirm. The shot is recorded with the rifle Hester carried first.</p>
        <p>17:01:30 — Sundip walks to the Power Core. The remaining three Frogs are at the south wall and visibly retreating. OWL's other knights are pursuing. There is no immediate combat threat. Sundip is alone in the center of the field. He climbs the Core's outer rim — a customary celebration that has been done by perhaps four Snipers in League history.</p>
        <p>17:01:32 — The Power Core overloads. The overload is uncontained. The Core had been latent for eight seasons. The League had not, in its eight-season memory, had the Core do this. The game systems engineering team will, in the offseason, release a report identifying the latent fault as a charge accumulation that had no release valve.</p>
        <p>17:01:33 — Sundip is killed by the overload. The dying Frog Speedster, who had been wounded by Sundip's shot, is also killed by the overload (the Speedster was within the blast radius and did not have time to retreat). Hester's Rifle falls at the base of the Core, on top of Sundip's body. Two of the three remaining Frogs are wounded but survive. OWL's other three knights are far enough away to be unaffected.</p>
        <p>17:01:55 — Round resolves with one OWL knight standing and two FRG knights standing. The round is declared a draw under the post-overload safety rule. The match goes to OWL on a 3-2 round count from the previous rounds, but the post-match writeup will note the draw round explicitly because the math depends on it. The Frogs will, the next morning, be given partial credit for "posthumously denying Sundip his celebration."</p>
        <p>17:03 — The OWL bench is silent. The FRG bench is silent. The Reno crowd is mostly silent. The lobster vendor in the concession area is, by sideline reporter account, weeping. The reporter does not name the vendor in the dispatch. The vendor is not the Brewer's Reach vendor. The vendor is a Reno concession worker on her first season of Cup-cycle work. She is recorded as saying, after composing herself: "I didn't know him. I just hate when this happens."</p>
        <p>17:04 — Hester's Rifle is photographed at the base of the Power Core by an LMG production assistant who has been told to retrieve it as soon as the field is cleared. The Rifle is retrieved at 17:11 and added to the post-match drop pile.</p>
        <p>17:30 — Cleanup begins. A vault steward, in the process of cataloging dropped gear, notices Hester's Rifle and recognizes it. The steward signs the dropped-gear ledger but does not turn the rifle in to the vault. The steward will, the next day, request unpaid leave. The League will grant the leave without comment. The Rifle has been at large since.</p>
        <p>17:45 — The League patches the Power Core overload latency in the post-season. The patch reduces the Core's charge accumulation cap by approximately forty percent and adds an audible warning at ninety percent. The patch is not retroactive. Sundip's death stands.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— LMG broadcast transcript · LY 8 R8 OWL vs FRG · expanded into formal match record at the family's request</div>
      </article>
    </div>

    <!-- ===== MUSINGS ===== -->
    <div class="lore-section" data-loresection="musings">
      <article class="musing">
        <h3>"On the Bow"</h3>
        <div class="meta">A musing · originally a fan letter, now widely cited</div>
        <p>The Wolves, after they kill, sometimes bow. This is not a rule. The Wolves did not always do this. The Wolves started doing this in Season 6, after the Final at Brewer's Reach, after the Healer.</p>
        <p>I do not think the Wolves are sorry. I think the Wolves think the dead are sacred. I think the Wolves are pack animals, and to a pack animal a fallen opponent is still part of the pattern of the pack. The bow is not an apology. The bow is an acknowledgment.</p>
        <p>This is why the bow has been adopted by no other team. The bow is wolf physics. It does not translate.</p>
        <p>I do not, however, think the Sharks should make fun of it.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— anonymous fan letter, reprinted in <em>The Long Bow</em>, vol. 2</div>
      </article>

      <article class="musing">
        <h3>"Why a Healer Wanders"</h3>
        <div class="meta">A musing · author: a former Healer who now writes for the Beaver paper</div>
        <p>People ask me, often, why I sometimes drifted across the field during my matches. They saw it on the broadcast. They thought it was poor coaching. It was not.</p>
        <p>A Healer's job is, technically, to heal the lowest-HP teammate. A Healer's actual job is to keep the team alive.</p>
        <p>Sometimes the lowest-HP teammate is a Tank, and the Tank is fine, because the Tank has plate. Sometimes the lowest-HP teammate is a Speedster, and the Speedster will not stay still long enough to be healed. Sometimes the lowest-HP teammate is in a position where, if I follow them, I will die. So I do not follow them. I follow someone else, or I drift toward the center to keep my options open. The drift is not laziness. The drift is patience.</p>
        <p>What I am trying to say is that a Healer who looks aimless is, in fact, doing the most thinking on the field. Snipers, please remember this. (You will not.)</p>
        <div class="signoff">— guest column, <em>The Lobster Reach Daily</em></div>
      </article>

      <article class="musing">
        <h3>"On the Long Marketing Group"</h3>
        <div class="meta">A musing · this is what the Beavers actually think · do not print</div>
        <p>The League is funded, in part, by an organization called the Long Marketing Group. They own the broadcast rights. Their slogan is "If it's not on LMG, it didn't happen."</p>
        <p>This is why some matches do not happen.</p>
        <p>I am writing this in confidence and you are not supposed to repeat it. The LMG decides which matches get coverage. The matches that do not get coverage are not in the standings. They are off the books. They are <em>training matches,</em> per the rule book. They are also, sometimes, real matches that the LMG simply chose not to broadcast, for reasons that are, you may imagine, not always good.</p>
        <p>The Sharks have lost three matches that were, on paper, training matches. The Beavers have won two of those. The standings do not reflect this. The standings reflect what the LMG broadcast.</p>
        <p>I am not saying we should burn down the LMG offices. I am saying that, if the LMG offices were to burn down accidentally, I would not personally call the fire engine on duty.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— anonymous, BVR coaching staff, "do not print"</div>
      </article>

      <article class="musing">
        <h3>"Concerning the Float Gas Tasters"</h3>
        <div class="meta">A musing · by someone who claims they were one</div>
        <p>I have been a Float Gas taster for the Boise Brawling Badgers for nine seasons. I am not a Badger. I will not say what I am. The Foundry pays me in cured salmon, twice a month, dropped at my back door in a box that does not have a return address.</p>
        <p>I taste each batch of Float Gas before it ships. I have tasted a lot of Float Gas. Float Gas tastes like cold pennies and a quiet song you used to know. Sometimes the song changes. When the song changes, I do not approve the batch. The Foundry then redoes the batch.</p>
        <p>I do not know what the song means. I do know that, in League Year 6, the song stopped, and the Foundry shut down, and the song came back, and the Foundry resumed. I do not have a theory about why. The Foundry has a theory. The Foundry has not shared it with me.</p>
        <p>I am writing this because someone should know. If I die in the next year, please tell my dog. The salmon is for the dog. The dog has a name. The Foundry knows it.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— anonymous, salmon delivery #2117</div>
      </article>

      <article class="musing">
        <h3>"On Permadeath"</h3>
        <div class="meta">A musing · by Coach Brask Ironclaw II, in the year of his son's death</div>
        <p>I lost my son this season. Brask the Third walked onto the field at Phoenix in Round 4 and did not walk off. He had three balloons. He used the third one finishing what he started. The League's report calls it "engagement complete." His mother and I call it Tuesday.</p>
        <p>I do not have a moral to share. People keep asking me what I have learned. I have learned nothing useful. I have learned that the League keeps every knight's helmet in a glass case in the Hall, and Brask's helmet is on a shelf next to his grandfather's, and the helmets <em>look the same</em>. I have learned that the Float Foundation will pay you for your kid in monthly installments and the installments are, in some technical sense, fair. I have learned that the Foxes have already drafted Brask's replacement.</p>
        <p>I have learned that the universe does not care that the Ironclaw line ended last Tuesday. The universe has a lot to do.</p>
        <p>I have not learned to forgive the Wolves. The Wolves did not kill him. The Wolves killed him three matches earlier in his career and he came back. I am going to spend the rest of my career not forgiving them anyway. The Wolves have offered to bow. They can keep their bow.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Brask Ironclaw II, FOX coaching staff, retirement letter</div>
      </article>

      <article class="musing">
        <h3>On Lobster</h3>
        <div class="meta">A musing · concession-row gossip · Boston, Brewer's Reach, ongoing</div>
        <p>The lobster vendor has been at Brewer's Reach since before the inaugural match. This is documented. There are photographs of the field on Match Day One, and the lobster booth is in them, already weathered, with a sign already missing the same letter (an "S," from "STEAMED"). The booth has not moved since. The vendor has not changed. She is older than every Beaver currently on roster. She is older than the Commissioner. She is, by some accounts, older than the founding paperwork.</p>
        <p>Foundation Day — the annual celebration of the inaugural match — is the only date the lobster vendor leaves Brewer's Reach. She catered the first Foundation Day in League Year 2 (apparently as a personal gesture; the LMG had not yet incorporated and had not yet asked her). She has catered every Foundation Day since. She returns the morning after, at the same hour, in the same coat, with no luggage. Where she stays during her single night away is a topic of recurring concession-row debate. The leading theories are: (a) Helena, but no one in Helena has ever seen her; (b) underwater with the Otters, but the Otters have never confirmed; (c) she does not stay anywhere — she walks the night out and arrives the morning after on foot. Theory (c) does not explain how she gets to League Foundation Hall in the first place.</p>
        <p>The Float Foundation pays its larger settlements in lobster. This is widely understood as a quirk of the Foundation's accounting and dismissed as eccentric. The Foundation receives its lobster from one supplier. The supplier is the Brewer's Reach vendor. The Foundation has no other lobster source. The Foundation's bookkeepers have, on three occasions, attempted to acquire a backup supplier; on three occasions the backup supplier has been found to have closed shop the next morning, with no explanation, and the lobsters in the secondary fridge spoiled overnight. The Foundation has stopped trying.</p>
        <p>The vendor's prices have not changed since LY 1. Three coppers for a lobster roll. Five for a steamed pair. Lobster bisque is "available, ask," which has meant, in practice, that you have to be somebody to get the bisque. Bristlebeard the First got the bisque. Bristlebeard the Second did not. The Commissioner gets the bisque. The Beavers' current Tank does not. Crash Wartooth, on his last visit to Boston, was offered the bisque without asking; he declined politely. He was killed three matches later. Beavers fans who track these things consider the bisque a death omen. The Wolves do not.</p>
        <p>Some fans believe the lobster vendor is immortal. Some fans are correct.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Concession-Row Gazette, columns 1 through 47, condensed</div>
      </article>

      <article class="musing">
        <h3>On the First Sixty Seconds</h3>
        <div class="meta">A musing · The Wing Standard, Quarterly, LY 8 follow-up column</div>
        <p>A month after the field-widening, I am writing this from a press box at the Reno Open Court. Below me, two teams are walking forward across a brown court that is now, by Rules Committee fiat, three hundred and twenty units wider than it was at the start of the season. The two teams have not yet engaged. Neither has yet fired a shot. They are walking. The crowd is, by my count, leaned forward.</p>
        <p>The first sixty seconds of every round used to be the dullest part of a Float Knights match. The Snipers fired. The Brawlers ran. The Healers crouched. By the thirty-second mark, two or three knights were already wounded, and the round's eventual shape was already drawn. The middle of the round was the best part. The first minute was filler. Coaches, in the off-season, have privately admitted as much.</p>
        <p>The wider field has rewritten this. The first sixty seconds are now the part of the round in which nothing has happened yet but everything is being decided. Where you put your Tank in the opening forty seconds determines whether your Sniper has a clean line at the eighty-second mark. Where you put your Healer in the opening twenty seconds determines whether you have a Healer at the ninety-second mark. The Brawlers, who used to charge straight at the center, now have to choose a lane. The Speedsters, who used to flank with reckless abandon, now have to time the flank. The decisions have not gotten harder. The decisions have gotten visible.</p>
        <p>I do not know if the crowd is leaning forward because the opening minute is now interesting, or because they have been told the opening minute is now interesting and are watching to confirm. Both are valid. The result is the same: the bleachers are quieter than they used to be, and the quiet is the kind that means people are paying attention.</p>
        <p>Below me, the Brawlers reach the center line. The Healers settle into position behind their Tanks. The first shot of the round goes off — a Sniper in red, fifty yards out, into a Healer's left flank. The wounded Healer does not retreat. They stand their ground and spend the round patching the teammates around them, taking three more hits, and dying with one balloon left at the seventy-second mark. The Sniper's team loses the round at the second-twenty-eight mark by failing to break the wounded line.</p>
        <p>The crowd is still leaning forward. They are louder now. The opening minute has stopped being filler.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Talia Featherquill, "The Bleachers Are Quiet," Wing Standard Quarterly, LY 8</div>
      </article>

      <article class="musing">
        <h3>The Healer's Creed</h3>
        <div class="meta">A musing · attributed to Dr. Lopecora Plumstone (HED Healer, founding generation) · LY 4, retransmitted</div>
        <p>I do not heal because I am kind. I heal because the alternative is unbearable. There is a difference, and the difference matters.</p>
        <p>A kind Healer would patch the knights they like and leave the knights they do not. A kind Healer would, in a hard moment, hesitate. A kind Healer would, watching a wounded enemy bleed out at the corner, let them. I have seen Healers like this. They retire early. They are not, by the time they retire, very kind anymore. The job has eaten the kindness out of them and left a careful, frightened person who cannot any longer tell whether they are doing right.</p>
        <p>I heal because I cannot watch a balloon pop. This is not metaphorical. I cannot watch the small hiss and the small slump and the small stillness afterward. The first time I watched it happen on the field — and it was a Wolf, in fact, an enemy, although I did not know yet that we had enemies — I walked from my position to theirs and patched them up before the round resolved. The Wolves complained. The League fined me. The League also, after some debate, codified the role I had just invented. I was, in that order: a Brawler with poor judgment, a problem, a precedent, a job description.</p>
        <p>The job description has gotten more refined since LY 1. Healers now know what they are. We are not Brawlers with poor judgment. We are the knights who walk toward the knights who are about to die, and we patch the leak before the hiss starts. We do this for our team. We do this for our enemies, when we can. We do this because the alternative — watching, doing nothing, kindly — is the worst version of being on the field.</p>
        <p>Train your Healers well. Pay them what you can. Do not ask them, in the locker room, whether they enjoy the job. They do not. They do it because the alternative is unbearable. That is the only reason that holds up under fire.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Dr. Lopecora Plumstone, "Notes Toward a Healer's Manual," LY 4 · republished by the Healers' Mutual Aid Society, LY 8</div>
      </article>
    </div>
    <div class="lore-section" data-loresection="timeline">
      <article class="history">
        <h3>The League Timeline</h3>
        <div class="meta">Maintained by the Float Foundation Archive · league years</div>
        <p>The events below use League Years (LY), counted from the founding of the Boston Beavers and the inaugural match at Brewer's Reach. The Collapse is in the unstated past; the League's current season is LY 8.</p>
        <div class="lore-timeline">
          <ol>
            <li><span class="yr">PRE-LEAGUE</span><div class="ev">The Collapse. North American power grids, banks, and telecommunications fail simultaneously. Cities reorganize around whatever they have. Boston rallies around its engineers. Tampa rallies around its salvage crews. Reno rallies around its slot machines. The Hedgehogs of Helena are already running backyard tournaments and pay no attention.</div></li>
            <li><span class="yr">LY 1</span><div class="ev">Boston Brisk Beavers founded. First match held at Brewer's Reach Pitch. The lobster vendor is already there. Old Bristlebeard the First dies defending the final round of the inaugural championship (S1 R5), taking three Beavers with him. His Riot Shield enters circulation as the league's first scavenged legacy item.</div></li>
            <li><span class="yr">LY 2</span><div class="ev">Wichita Wild Wolves founded. The Pack arrives. The Bow ritual is observed informally.</div></li>
            <li><span class="yr">LY 3</span><div class="ev">Tampa Tornado Sharks and Boise Brawling Badgers founded. The Foundry begins distilling Float Gas. Tomik Splashfang the Bold (SHK Brawl, S3 R2) fumbles a frag grenade in Tampa's third match ever and takes two Beavers with him. The Sharks retire his number for the season.</div></li>
            <li><span class="yr">LY 4</span><div class="ev">Phoenix Furious Foxes and Kansas Kicking Kits founded. Commissioner Whiskerstone takes office. The Long Marketing Group is incorporated, securing exclusive broadcast rights from a single salvaged Tampa tower. Brask Ironclaw III (FOX Striker, S4 R4) dies at Phoenix; his father retires from coaching.</div></li>
            <li><span class="yr">LY 5</span><div class="ev">Seattle Sleepless Owls and Fresno Frantic Frogs founded. Old Bristlebeard the Second (BVR Tank, S5 R1) walks into Otter fire on the opening kickoff of the season. The Beavers do not field a Bristlebeard for two years.</div></li>
            <li><span class="yr">LY 5</span><div class="ev">Vela Hootington (OWL Speed, S5 R7) dies holding a 22-kill streak in OWL's inaugural season. The next two knights to attempt to break the streak die in the same round trying. Mira Pawington (KIT Speed, S5 R6) dies completing her career as the Scavenger Queen, with her opponent's Power Cell still in her hand.</div></li>
            <li><span class="yr">LY 5</span><div class="ev">First Coronation Cup. The brass crown is forged by Pell Whittlestone of Boise and delivered to the Commissioner in a borrowed pillowcase. The first winner is RCC. The crown is reported missing the morning after; it is recovered, with difficulty, three weeks later.</div></li>
            <li><span class="yr">LY 6</span><div class="ev">Portland Punching Pandas and Olympia Outlaw Otters founded. The Boise Float Gas Foundry stops production for forty-one days. No explanation given. The Badgers play with reserve gas and finish 2-12. An unsigned letter arrives at the League Office; nobody acknowledges it.</div></li>
            <li><span class="yr">LY 6</span><div class="ev">Bristlebeard's Riot Shield is lost on the field at the season's final, en route to the vault. The Shield disappears in transit. The Reno Heist begins. The Beavers are still angry.</div></li>
            <li><span class="yr">LY 6</span><div class="ev">First Pawington Cup. Mira Pawington's accidental flag-carry vs the Otters becomes the basis for a formal team event; the Commissioner names the Cup after her while she is still alive. The first official Cup is held later that season at Hardwood Hall and won by KIT.</div></li>
            <li><span class="yr">LY 7</span><div class="ev">Reno Rascal Raccoons founded. Crash Wartooth (WLF Brawl, S6 R3 — recently posthumous) is stunned three times in twelve seconds and dies; the Crash Rule is enacted league-wide. The Stadium Wars begin: Reno tries to buy Brewer's Reach, Tampa tries to buy the Foundry, Helena tells everyone "no" forty-eight times. Whiskerstone settles the dispute by sitting on every disputed deed for one consecutive month.</div></li>
            <li><span class="yr">LY 7</span><div class="ev">The Marshmallow Incident. Felix Marshmallow (PND Tank, S7 R5) is killed by friendly grenade fire. The Marshmallow Protocol is enacted; pre-season grenade safety drills are now mandatory.</div></li>
            <li><span class="yr">LY 7</span><div class="ev">First official Hallowtide Tag. The Wolves' informal pack-bonding ritual is codified at the Commissioner's office at Wikrai Wartooth Jr.'s formal request: "Because Crash never got to play it." The inaugural Tag is won by Kor Wartooth in seventy-eight seconds.</div></li>
            <li><span class="yr">LY 8</span><div class="ev">Helena Hidden Hedgehogs founded — the newest formal League team, though the Hedgehogs as a faction have been fighting in informal tournaments for generations. Hester Quillstrike (HED Sniper) makes her debut at the Helena Glacier Rink and, in HED's third match (S8 R3), sets the 412-pace longest-shot record. She dies in the same match to a coordinated four-Otter rush. Her Railgun Rifle is scavenged.</div></li>
            <li><span class="yr">LY 8</span><div class="ev">Sundip Hootington Jr. (OWL Sniper, S8 R8) carrying Hester's Rifle, ties the 412-pace record at the Reno Open Court — matching it exactly without breaking it. He climbs the Power Core to celebrate. The Power Core overloads. He does not survive. The Frog Speedster he had just shot also does not survive. The League patches the overload that off-season. Hester's Rifle falls at the base of the Core and is scavenged in cleanup.</div></li>
            <li><span class="yr">LY 8</span><div class="ev">Reka Pawington (KIT Speed, rookie) sets the Pawington Cup single-match cap record with three captures vs the Sharks. The Pawington Arc — her unusually wide flag-running route — is widely copied and never replicated. Kor Wartooth wins his second consecutive Hallowtide Tag with all three balloons intact. The LY 8 Coronation Cup goes to RCC for the sixth time; the crown is, again, reported missing the morning after.</div></li>
            <li><span class="yr">LY 8</span><div class="ev">The Personal Sandbag enters the bettor's storefront. The Knights' Union petitions for a notification system. The Commissioner declines. The Wing Standard editorializes. The item remains for sale. The League's Ethics Committee files a formal opinion that the item is "sabotage with a price tag." The opinion does not change policy. Both sides claim the moral high ground.</div></li>
            <li><span class="yr">LY 8</span><div class="ev">The Reno Cultural Office paper Project Neon Inheritance leaks via a junior intern. The paper had been internal-only and described in detail how Reno's three-block lower-Strip survival was a direct consequence of one continuous slot-machine power line during the Collapse. The Cultural Office does not comment. The leak does not damage anyone. The League Library quietly accessions a copy.</div></li>
            <li><span class="yr">LY 8</span><div class="ev">The Rules Committee announces the field-widening: from 960×540 units to 1280×720 units, with spawn lines repositioned to one thousand eighty units apart — beyond maximum effective range of any current ranged weapon. The change is justified as restoring a tactical opening phase. The Wolves are unhappy. The Otters are euphoric. Talia Featherquill writes a column. The League adapts.</div></li>
            <li><span class="yr">CURRENT</span><div class="ev">The League continues. The Riot Shield is somewhere. The Foundry hums. The Wolves bow. The Healers wander. The Snipers shoot the Healers. The Float Foundation pays out in lobster, in installments. The lobster vendor is still there. The 412-pace record is still co-held, posthumously, by two snipers who never met. The field is wider now. The opening minute is slower. The crowd has not stopped noticing.</div></li>
          </ol>
        </div>
      </article>
    </div>

    <!-- ===== GLOSSARY ===== -->
    <div class="lore-section" data-loresection="glossary">
      <article class="history lore-glossary">
        <h3>Glossary of Terms</h3>
        <div class="meta">Reference · maintained by the League Office Editorial Board</div>
        <dl>
          <dt>The 412-Pixel Mark</dt>
          <dd>The League's longest single-shot kill on record, measured in arena paces from the firing position. Set by Hester Quillstrike (HED Sniper) at the Helena Glacier Rink in HED's inaugural season (LY 8 R3). Tied — exactly, never broken — by Sundip Hootington Jr. (OWL Sniper) at the Reno Open Court later that same year. The mark is now jointly held, posthumously, by both shooters.</dd>

          <dt>The Balloon Contract</dt>
          <dd>The standard agreement signed by every Knight before joining a roster. Specifies that a Knight is allotted three balloons per match, popped one-per-knockout. The third pop ends the Knight's match (and, after enough cumulative pops across their career, ends the Knight). Allegedly proposed by Splinter Tooth in League Year 1.</dd>

          <dt>The Bayou Walk</dt>
          <dd>The Tigers' pre-match ritual, first introduced at the LY 9 founding bell. The four Tiger knights of the active line walk the perimeter of the Bayou Cypress-Hollow venue once before the opening bell, in a single column, slowly, without speaking. The matriarch (Stripeshoulder, as of LY 9) walks at the front. The Walk is not, by the Tigers' own account, a strategic element. It is a way of greeting the field. The Tigers continue the practice at away matches when the host venue permits it; most do.</dd>

          <dt>The Bow</dt>
          <dd>A Wolf-pack ritual, first observed at Brewer's Reach in Season 6. After a fallen opponent goes down, the Wolves on the field briefly form a circle around the body and the captain bows. Not adopted by any other team. The Wolves themselves consider it a form of acknowledgment, not apology.</dd>

          <dt>The Collapse</dt>
          <dd>The single-night event that preceded the League's founding. Power grids, banking, and telecommunications failed simultaneously across most of the continent; the prior dominant species — who had built the cities, the wireless, the rune-glyphs in concrete — were, by the second week, no longer present. The League takes no official position on whether they died or went somewhere not followed. The League does not require any Knight to have an opinion on the Collapse to play.</dd>

          <dt>Concession Row</dt>
          <dd>The strip of food vendors along the eastern boundary of Brewer's Reach Pitch, Boston. Notable for the lobster vendor who has been there since before the inaugural match (LY 1) and has not, for any reason, ever moved her booth. Her prices have not changed since the League's founding. She is the Float Foundation's sole lobster supplier.</dd>

          <dt>The Coronation Cup</dt>
          <dd>The League's annual single-match crown event, held at the Reno Open Court on Foundation Day. A brass crown (forged LY 5 by Pell Whittlestone of Boise) spawns at the center of the arena; whoever wears it scores team points per second but cannot fire a weapon. First team to fifty points wins. RCC has won six of eight. The crown has been reported "missing for inspection" the morning after every RCC win and recovered, every time, in a Reno pawn shop. The League's Atlas Committee has stopped commenting.</dd>

          <dt>The Denver Stand</dt>
          <dd>The Bison's home-match tradition: the patriarch (Yellowhide, as of LY 8) stands at the south end of the Highland-Plains pitch in full ceremonial harness for the duration of every home match. Not a coaching role. The herd-rotation tactical style requires every Bison knight to know where the herd's center of gravity is at all moments; Yellowhide is, by tradition, that gravity. Opposing teams have learned to orient on him too — by the third pre-League exhibition match, every visiting knight had unconsciously adjusted their positioning relative to his standing point.</dd>

          <dt>The Crash Rule</dt>
          <dd>A Wolves' Brawler-only equipment rule, named for Crash Wartooth, requiring all Wolves Brawlers to wear heavy plate. Adopted in Season 7 after Wartooth was stunned three times in twelve seconds.</dd>

          <dt>Felix Drills</dt>
          <dd>The unofficial in-locker-room name for the Marshmallow Protocol's grenade-safety training. Mandatory league-wide since Season 7.</dd>

          <dt>The Float Foundation</dt>
          <dd>A neutral non-profit jointly funded by the League and the LMG. Operates the Hall of Fallen Heroes, pays out benefits to surviving family of permadeath Knights (often in lobster, by family request), and runs the Foundation Hall venue for Foundation Day events.</dd>

          <dt>Float Gas</dt>
          <dd>The proprietary lifting gas that fills League-spec balloons. Distilled at the Boise Float Gas Foundry. Each team receives a different formula: Boise gets the heaviest batch (with regulators); Olympia gets the lightest; Phoenix gets the desert formula (high-altitude expansion); etc. Composition is patented and undisclosed. The Foundry shut down for 41 days in League Year 6 with no explanation.</dd>

          <dt>The Float Gas Tasters</dt>
          <dd>A small group of (allegedly non-Badger) individuals retained by the Foundry to taste each batch of Float Gas before it ships. Paid in cured salmon. Their job is to detect "the song" — an undefined sensory quality of approved Float Gas.</dd>

          <dt>Foundation Day</dt>
          <dd>Annual league holiday celebrating the inaugural match at Brewer's Reach. The Holiday Festive (League Foundation Hall) biome is in rotation during this event.</dd>

          <dt>The Founding Years</dt>
          <dd>League Years 1 through 8 — the period during which the League's twelve formal teams were progressively admitted (BVR in LY 1, HED in LY 8). Most of the heroes in the Hall of Fallen died during these years, and most of the league's institutional rules (the Crash Rule, the Marshmallow Protocol, the Stadium Wars settlement, the Riot Shield's seven-carrier saga) were established in this period.</dd>

          <dt>Hallowtide</dt>
          <dd>League-wide week-long autumnal event. The Halloween (Reno Hallowtide Hollow) biome is the host venue. Wichita Wolves famously play their best games during Hallowtide.</dd>

          <dt>Hallowtide Tag</dt>
          <dd>The League's only projectile-banned event. Played at the Reno Hallowtide Hollow during Hallowtide week. One knight is "It"; the others flee. Tagging requires a melee strike and freezes the target for three seconds, during which they take heavy bleed damage. The "It" role does not pass — the tagger keeps it. The match ends when only one knight remains alive; ranking is by death order. The Wolves have won every official Hallowtide Tag (codified LY 7). Kor Wartooth is the two-time defending champion. The Pandas have entered every year and have never finished above tenth.</dd>

          <dt>The Hedgehog Tradition</dt>
          <dd>The informal Helena combat circuit that predates HED's formal League admission by several generations. Backyard ranges, neighborhood three-on-three brawls, and mountain-pass exhibition matches gave Hedgehog snipers in particular a sustained training environment outside League oversight. This is why Hester Quillstrike, a rookie in HED's inaugural season (LY 8), was already capable of setting the league's longest-shot record (the 412-Pixel Mark) in her debut year.</dd>

          <dt>The Long Marketing Group (LMG)</dt>
          <dd>Post-Collapse advertising consortium that owns the League's broadcast rights. Slogan: "If it's not on LMG, it didn't happen." Famously antagonistic with the Commissioner's office. Funds half of the Float Foundation in exchange for tax write-offs.</dd>

          <dt>The Marshmallow Protocol</dt>
          <dd>League-wide grenade safety regulation enacted after the Marshmallow Incident in Season 7 of LY 7. Mandates pre-season grenade-safety drills for all teams. Officially named for Felix Marshmallow; informally called "Felix Drills."</dd>

          <dt>The Murder of Pittsburgh</dt>
          <dd>The collective name for the Raven settlement built into the foundry ruins of post-Collapse Pittsburgh. ("Murder" is the corvid collective noun; the Ravens use it without irony.) The Murder is governed by an elder-council known only as the Roost; major decisions require consensus among nine elders. The Pittsburgh Plundering Ravens are the Murder's official League representation. The Murder, as a body, does not bury its dead — cremation in the foundry-fires has been their practice for as long as anyone has counted, including before the Collapse.</dd>

          <dt>The Antlerwood Rule</dt>
          <dd>League rule, ratified LY 9 founding bell. Designates non-removable bony growths — specifically antlers, horns, and tusks — as <em>attached weapons</em> for the purposes of melee resolution. Named for Bertaud Antlerwood, whose six-foot-four-inch antler span at the LY 9 Caucus's pre-season measurement triggered the original ruling. The rule applies League-wide but, in practice, governs primarily the Moose and the Bison (the latter through a separate clause for horns). The Sharks, who proposed an analogous rule for teeth, were politely deferred on the grounds that teeth are removable.</dd>

          <dt>The Desert Pattern</dt>
          <dd>The Sonora Sly Coyotes' signature engagement tactic, in which one Coyote appears to retreat — repeatedly — drawing opposing knights into believing the Coyote position is collapsing, until six Coyotes engage simultaneously from positions the opposing team had thought were already cleared. The pattern requires precise timing across the entire pack, and has been drilled (per the Sonora pack's training records) eleven thousand times. Coined by Featherquill in her LY 9 founding-bell preview feature; the Coyotes themselves describe the tactic as <em>"asking the desert."</em></dd>

          <dt>The Lynx Solitude</dt>
          <dd>The Yukon Hunting Lynx training tradition, in which each active knight trains alone with a single position-coach for the bulk of the season; the team only forms up on match day. The five active Lynx knights have five separate position-coaches; the team's senior coach (Tessik Snowstep, as of LY 9) reviews training but does not direct it. The League considers the practice unusual but compliant — the rule book requires teams to <em>have</em> training programs, not to specify what they look like.</dd>

          <dt>The Quetzal Nest</dt>
          <dd>The rafter-nest maintained personally by Cintzli of the Yucatan canopy in the Yucatan Jungle-Canopy venue's overgrown rafters. The nest is not formally registered as terrain but functions, in matches at the Yucatan venue, as Cintzli's preferred shooting position. The Caucus has discussed registering the nest on three separate occasions and has, on each occasion, declined to register it on the grounds that doing so would imply the League had built it (the League did not build it; Cintzli built it; the rafters were there before either). The Quetzals have not asked for the nest to be registered; the Caucus has not asked it to be removed; the matter is, by mutual silent agreement, not a matter.</dd>

          <dt>The Northwalk</dt>
          <dd>The journey the Anchorage Polar Bears completed on foot in the LY 8 mid-season — from Anchorage to Brewer's Reach in thirty-two days — to deliver their inclusion application to the League in person. The Polar Bears do not consider the Northwalk a feat. They consider it the courtesy that an application of that gravity required. The League considers it remarkable. Whitepaw, on arrival, is reported to have asked only for water and a chair before reading the application aloud to Whiskerstone. Whiskerstone read the application in silence afterward. The audio of Whitepaw's reading, captured by Treadwell at Whiskerstone's request, is in LMG archive vault 2. It has been requested for public broadcast eleven times. It has been released zero times.</dd>

          <dt>The Pawington Tradition</dt>
          <dd>A Kits-only scavenging ritual where the scavenger kneels by the fallen and asks permission before taking gear. Originated with Mira Pawington. Bench officials have on at least one occasion claimed to hear the corpse <em>respond</em>; that bench official was sent home.</dd>

          <dt>The Pawington Arc</dt>
          <dd>An unusually wide arcing run from flag-pickup back to home base, used by Reka Pawington (Mira's first cousin once removed) during Pawington Cup matches. The arc adds approximately 30% to the direct route distance but consistently evades defenders, who anticipate a straight-line return. Three other Speedsters across the League have attempted to copy the Arc. None have replicated Reka's cap rate. Reka has, on multiple occasions, attributed the Arc to advice received from her late cousin: "the corners watch you. The middle doesn't care." League historians do not know what this means.</dd>

          <dt>The Pawington Cup</dt>
          <dd>The League's flag-running team event, codified LY 6 by Commissioner Whiskerstone after a Mira-vs-Otters incident in which Mira accidentally invented the format. A flag spawns at center; teams compete to carry it back to their own base. The carrier cannot fire and moves at four-fifths speed. First team to five captures wins. Hosted on alternating years by KIT (Hardwood Hall) and BVR (Brewer's Reach Pitch). KIT has won five Cups. BVR has won two. SHK has entered three and won zero, having refused on principle to slow to four-fifths speed.</dd>

          <dt>Permadeath</dt>
          <dd>The condition triggered when a Knight uses up the last of their three lives in a match. Permadeath is final. The Knight's gear drops on the field for survivors to scavenge; their helmet is placed in the Hall of Fallen Heroes; their family receives Float Foundation benefits.</dd>

          <dt>The Reno Heist</dt>
          <dd>The presumed (officially: alleged, possibly imagined) theft of Bristlebeard's Riot Shield in transit to the vault after Season 6's final. The Shield has since been seen on a Raccoon roster, then stolen back, then re-stolen, then re-stolen. Its current location is unknown and is being looked for. The lookers are tired.</dd>

          <dt>Reserve Gas</dt>
          <dd>The lighter, alternative Float Gas formula the Boise Foundry distributed during the LY 6 forty-one-day shutdown. Reserve gas burns cleaner but lifts less; matches played on it have a slightly different feel that veterans claim to detect. The Badgers, who normally receive the heaviest formula and rely on it, finished the LY 6 season 2-12. Reserve Gas is not currently in production. Its formula is not, officially, known to anyone outside the Foundry's senior chemist; unofficially it is widely suspected of being known to whoever wrote the unsigned Foundry letter that arrived in LY 6.</dd>

          <dt>Scavenging</dt>
          <dd>Picking up gear from a fallen Knight on the field. Scavenged gear retains its previous owner's name in the lineage chain (e.g., "Vela's Plate Cuirass"). Items with high scavenge counts become legendary; the most-scavenged item in League history is Bristlebeard's Riot Shield (7 carriers across 6 seasons).</dd>

          <dt>The Stadium Wars</dt>
          <dd>The Season 7 inter-city dispute when Reno, Tampa, and Fresno tried to buy each other's home stadiums. Resolved by Commissioner Whiskerstone, who sat on every contested deed for one consecutive month. Established the permanent League rule that stadiums cannot be sold; this is why neutral venues exist.</dd>

          <dt>The Steward's Pocket</dt>
          <dd>Locker-room shorthand for the bizarre incident in the LY 6 vault rotation, when a steward repeatedly arrived at the vault with a sealed bag containing one fewer item than the bag had been sealed with. The phrase "steward's pocket" now describes any object that goes missing without rational explanation.</dd>

          <dt>Tactical Erasure</dt>
          <dd>Sniper jargon, originating with the Tampa Sharks scouts' shared journal: a kill on a Healer that determines the outcome of an engagement before the engagement has even begun. Many Snipers find the term distasteful but most consider it accurate.</dd>

          <dt>Tactical Self-Sacrifice</dt>
          <dd>The League's official designation for the act of intentionally absorbing fatal damage to deny the kill or its effect to the opposing team. First codified after Old Bristlebeard the First ate a grenade in the inaugural championship final to deny it to the Wolves. Counts as a normal kill in the stats but is decorated separately in the Hall.</dd>

          <dt>The Coalbroke Stop</dt>
          <dd>A bathroom break in transit between the LY 6 final and the League's neutral vault, during which Bristlebeard's Riot Shield is generally agreed to have disappeared from a sealed bag in a parked truck. The truck driver bought a soda. The gas station camera does not point at the truck. Three reporters and one detective have asked. None of them have answers. Locker-room shorthand for "the moment the thing was actually stolen, even though officially nothing was."</dd>

          <dt>Focus Fire</dt>
          <dd>A team tactic, descended from competitive shooter and MOBA play, in which all team members coordinate to attack a single enemy target at the same time. The League's broadcast booth introduced the term in LY 7 after the Owls demonstrated it in a semifinal against the Beavers — five Owl knights independently selected the same wounded Beavers Tank and converged on him within a single round. The Tank had 38 HP. The kill resolved in 1.4 seconds. The Beavers' coach called it "uncomfortably professional." The League's response was to let it happen.</dd>

          <dt>Hold Range</dt>
          <dd>A Sniper's preferred firing distance, generally seventy to ninety percent of their weapon's maximum range. Closer than seventy and they're vulnerable to brawlers; further than ninety and damage falloff makes the shot unreliable. Snipers in Hold Range are said to be "in pocket." Snipers below it are said to be "out of pocket," which is the same expression the Beavers locker room uses to describe knights who are about to embarrass themselves.</dd>

          <dt>Personal Sandbag</dt>
          <dd>A storefront item, introduced LY 8, costing one hundred and fifty bits, applying a fractional speed reduction to one named opposing knight for the duration of a single match. Subject to a long-running ethics debate; the League's official position is that the item is legal sabotage, the Knights' Union's position is that it is sabotage that knights cannot defend against because they cannot detect it, and The Wing Standard's position is that both of these are correct simultaneously.</dd>

          <dt>The Phoenix Heat Clause</dt>
          <dd>A scheduling rule, negotiated LY 3 and revised LY 7, requiring Phoenix-hosted matches between June and mid-September to either start after 7:30pm local OR run under the misting system installed at Skyline Pitch. The clause is responsible for the orange-pink sky that appears in most Foxes home broadcasts, and for the faint soda smell at the field's edge.</dd>

          <dt>Rally Point</dt>
          <dd>The position toward which a wounded knight retreats when their team has a healer or a healthy Tank in the field. Distinct from "running away" — a rally point implies a destination, and a destination implies a plan. The League's broadcast team uses the term to distinguish controlled retreats from panicked ones. Coaches use the term to distinguish a knight who is doing their job from a knight who is not.</dd>

          <dt>Sandbagged Knight</dt>
          <dd>A knight who is performing measurably below their stat sheet for reasons unrelated to injury, fatigue, or known field conditions. The most common cause, since LY 8, is the Personal Sandbag item. The term predates the item — it was originally used to describe knights whose teams had quietly arranged for them to lose, in order to extend the franchise's playoff window. The Commissioner's Office prefers the post-LY-8 usage and has discouraged the older sense.</dd>

          <dt>Pawington Arc</dt>
          <dd>An unusually wide flag-running route, named for Reka Pawington (KIT, rookie LY 8) who used it to set the Pawington Cup's single-match capture record at three captures. The arc takes the carrier near the field's edges instead of straight back to base, adding roughly 30% to the route distance. Defenders consistently fail to anticipate it. Three other Speedsters have attempted the arc; none have replicated the cap rate. Reka has declined to explain why the arc works for her and not for the imitators. Her stated reason is a quote from her aunt Mira: <em>"Mira always said the corners watch you. The middle doesn't care."</em> League historians do not know what this means.</dd>

          <dt>Project Neon Inheritance</dt>
          <dd>The Reno Cultural Office's internal working paper documenting how Reno survived the Collapse. The paper's central finding: a single dedicated power line on the lower Strip stayed lit when the rest of the city went dark, because that line had been installed by the casino consortium specifically to keep slot machines spinning during outages. Three blocks. Twelve buildings. The Raccoons moved in early; food trade organized along the lit blocks within a month; engineering guilds gathered in the slot-machine bays within five years. The Cultural Office classified the paper internal-only. A junior intern photocopied it in LY 8 and leaked it to The Wing Standard. The Cultural Office has never commented. The intern is now a junior columnist at the same paper. The leak did not damage anyone, the League Library quietly accessioned a copy, and the Cultural Office continues to write polished sentences for orientation pamphlets.</dd>

          <dt>Spawn-Camping</dt>
          <dd>(historical, deprecated since LY 8.) The practice of firing on the opposing spawn line from one's own spawn line at the moment of round commencement. Possible because the original LY 1 field dimensions assumed ranged weapons of the era had effective ranges shorter than the spawn-to-spawn distance. The development of the Railgun in LY 5 invalidated the assumption. Spawn-camping was, for several seasons thereafter, a legal but widely-criticized opening tactic. The LY 8 field-widening — which placed the spawn lines one thousand eighty units apart, two hundred and twenty units beyond the maximum effective range of any current ranged weapon — eliminated the tactic. Coaches now refer to it in past tense, with mild relief.</dd>

          <dt>The Field-Widening</dt>
          <dd>The LY 8 mid-season expansion of the standard match field from 960×540 units to 1280×720 units. The change was announced by Rules Committee bulletin in plain, mildly embarrassed language. Its purpose was to eliminate spawn-camping (see above) and restore a tactical opening phase to each round. The Wolves were unhappy. The Otters were euphoric. Talia Featherquill wrote a column for The Wing Standard. The LMG broadcast team adapted their camera framing within one match. Knights adapted their opening positioning within one season. The field-widening is, by general consensus, the single most consequential rules change in League history that did not require a death to motivate it.</dd>

          <dt>The Crown's Engravings</dt>
          <dd>The Coronation Cup crown was forged by Pell Whittlestone (BDG, retired LY 4) at the close of LY 4 and engraved at her workshop in Boise vernacular script. The League's calligraphers can identify the script as Boise but cannot fully translate it beyond the obvious team-emblem motifs. Whittlestone has declined three formal League requests to translate the engravings, on the grounds that the engravings "are not for the League." A new sigil is added to the rim each season, between Cups. The crown is mailed to Whittlestone after the season closes; she returns it before the next Cup. The League pays in lobster. The accounting is sealed. The current crown carries six sigils — one for each Cup champion — and an additional set of small marks along the inner band that have never appeared in any photograph.</dd>

          <dt>Lobster Standard</dt>
          <dd>The Float Foundation's persistent practice of paying its larger settlements in lobster instead of bits. Treated as eccentric by outside observers and as accounting fact by the Foundation's bookkeepers. The Foundation has one supplier — the Brewer's Reach lobster vendor — and has, on three documented occasions, attempted to acquire a backup supplier; on three occasions the backup supplier has closed shop the next morning, with no explanation, and the lobsters in the secondary fridge have spoiled overnight. The Foundation has stopped trying. Pell Whittlestone (the crown forger) bills exclusively in lobster. So does at least one independent contractor at the Boise Foundry. So does, by implication and the records of three settled lawsuits, the Wing Standard's own publication office. The Lobster Standard is, by every reasonable measure, the League's secondary currency. The Commissioner has not formally acknowledged this. The Commissioner's ledgers have.</dd>

          <dt>Bristlebeard Pattern</dt>
          <dd>The well-documented Beaver Tank tradition in which the team's anchoring Tank takes the surname Bristlebeard, charges directly at the first enemy line they see, and dies in their first or second season. The First died in LY 1 R5, taking three opposing Beavers with him. The Second died on the opening kickoff of LY 5. The Third — Bjorn Bristlebeard, currently active — has, as of this writing, broken the pattern by playing a defensive back line and surviving eight matches into LY 8. The Beaver fanbase is split between those who want the pattern restored and those who want Bjorn to live. Bjorn is twenty-two. Bjorn carries the First's Riot Shield. The Foundation has offered to vault the Shield. Bjorn has declined. The fanbase notices.</dd>
        </dl>
      </article>
    </div>

  </div>
</div>

<!-- ============== ABOUT TAB ============== -->
<div class="tab-content" data-tab="about">
  <div class="about-page">
    <h2>ℹ️ About Float Knights</h2>
    <div class="sub">An auto-playing mascot tournament sim. Cute, cuddly <b>dodgeball but with guns</b> — that's the closest description. Cute animals in medieval armor wielding futuristic weapons. HP = balloons. Death is permanent (eventually).</div>

    <div class="credits">
      <div class="block">
        <h3>How it works</h3>
        <p><b>20 teams</b> in the league as of LY 9 mid-season (12 founders + 4 LY 8 closeout expansion teams: Tigers, Polar Bears, Bison, Ravens + 4 LY 9 mid-season Continental Expansion teams: Lynx, Moose, Coyotes, Quetzals). Each season randomly draws 12 teams into the qualifier-then-eight-team ladder — 4 qualifier matches → 4 quarterfinals → 2 semifinals → final. Bracket size is configurable down to 4 in Settings. Each match is best-of-5 rounds. Each round is a <b>7-on-7</b> elimination fight (Healers and Grenadiers joined the original five archetypes in v22).</p>
        <p>A round averages around 40-45 seconds. A full match runs 3-5 minutes. Bets are placed between rounds.</p>
        <p>Knights have <b>3 lives per match</b>. When KO'd in a round, they respawn the next round — <b>unless they've used all 3 lives</b>, in which case they die permanently and their gear drops on the field for survivors to scavenge. Scavenged gear keeps its previous owner's name and accumulates a lineage chain across the league's history.</p>
      </div>

      <div class="block">
        <h3>The seven archetypes</h3>
        <ul style="line-height:1.7;">
          <li><b>🛡️ Tank</b> — heavy plate, slow movement, soaks hits. Doesn't dodge.</li>
          <li><b>⚔️ Striker</b> — balanced offense, the tournament's workhorse class.</li>
          <li><b>💨 Speedster</b> — squishy, fast, the best dodger on the field.</li>
          <li><b>👊 Brawler</b> — close-quarters specialist with crit potential.</li>
          <li><b>🎯 Sniper</b> — long-range, glass cannon. Snipers preferentially target healers.</li>
          <li><b>✚ Healer</b> — support unit. Heals the lowest-HP teammate over time. Has a minimum range (won't melee), so it physically follows whoever it's healing. May wander across the battlefield when switching heal targets.</li>
          <li><b>💣 Grenadier</b> — throws ONE grenade at the start of an engagement, dealing AoE damage. Then falls back to weak melee — bring 'em early.</li>
        </ul>
      </div>

      <div class="block">
        <h3>What the AI actually does</h3>
        <ul style="line-height:1.7;">
          <li><b>Tactical formations</b> — six pre-positioning patterns (line, wedge, phalanx, echelon, killbox, flying-V) selected per team per round. Roles fill slots by tactical priority — tanks anchor the front (or center, in the killbox), healers trail safely, speedsters lead the flying-V's point. Different formations match up differently, so the opening of a round actually matters now.</li>
          <li>Shows mercy to low-HP enemies; flees on low HP toward health pickups</li>
          <li>Hunts down attackers (mercy state); swarms toward visible enemies</li>
          <li>Picks up and uses items, scavenges fallen gear if it's an upgrade</li>
          <li>Navigates around terrain blocks (corner-stuck strafe to round walls)</li>
          <li><b>Dodges incoming projectiles</b> — Speedsters dodge often, Snipers rarely, Tanks not at all. Per-knight cooldown so dodges punctuate, not dominate. Low-HP knights in melee can dodge BACKWARDS to disengage from scrums.</li>
          <li>Random crits and misses; weapon inaccuracy creates natural variance</li>
          <li>Snipers don't camp the corner anymore; they kite to ~50% of weapon range</li>
          <li>Pushes the center for the Power Core when a teammate is closest</li>
          <li>Knockback on every hit, scaled by damage and reduced by armor</li>
        </ul>
      </div>

      <div class="block">
        <h3>Match details</h3>
        <ul style="line-height:1.7;">
          <li>Player names are <b>massively</b> randomized (~4.1 billion combinations) — you won't see a duplicate twice in a lifetime</li>
          <li>Projectiles have physics: velocity, range, swept collision, pierce-prevention through walls</li>
          <li>Knights, terrain, and projectiles all collide; dropped items have hitboxes</li>
          <li>Field obstacle placement is randomized per match (Power Core gets a guaranteed clear zone in the center)</li>
          <li><b>1,920 stadium variations</b> — generated by a three-axis factory: 20 cities × 8 sports × 12 effect overlays (Stock + Foundry + Underdig + Submerged + Nightwoods + Hallowtide + Petalfall + Frostfall + Neonspark + Bramble + Aurora + Harvest). Each combination is visually distinct: a Phoenix Sandstone Foundry Pitch doesn't look like a Yukon Tundra-Silver Submerged Pitch. Stadium picked per match; full preview catalog browsable in the Lore tab with an effect-picker for the 12 variants.</li>
          <li>Match outcomes are <b>seeded and procedurally generated</b> — same seed reproduces the same match exactly, every time</li>
          <li>Sim speed is adjustable (bottom-right of the arena)</li>
          <li>The bottom <b>stock-ticker</b> rolls live scores, top streaks, lost-loot callouts, and recent feed events</li>
        </ul>
      </div>

      <div class="block">
        <h3>The economy</h3>
        <p>You earn "bits" by placing bets on matches you watch. Bet markets: Match Winner, Round Winner, Last Knight Standing, First Kill, Total Casualties (over/under), Match Length (over/under).</p>
        <p>Spend bits at the Store — three rarity tiers (Common, Rare, Legendary) of buffs and debuffs. Sandbag the opponent's whole team, curse them with -10 HP, or splash an Elixir on your roster for the legendary treatment.</p>
      </div>

      <div class="block">
        <h3>Inspirations</h3>
        <ul>
          <li><i>Blaseball</i> — permanent mortality + emergent narrative</li>
          <li><i>Mario Kart 64 Battle Mode</i> — balloons as HP</li>
          <li><i>Worms</i> — death cries on KO + gear pickup chaos</li>
          <li><i>Salem / UO</i> — items named after their dead carriers</li>
        </ul>
      </div>

      <div class="block">
        <h3>Roadmap (still cooking)</h3>
        <ul>
          <li><b>AI sportscaster commentary</b> — live narration of the action via the ai-text-plugin</li>
          <li><b>Replay viewer</b> — re-watch any past match (made possible by the deterministic seeded sim)</li>
          <li><b>Cross-user shared league</b> — every visitor sees the same match in progress at the same time, via uploadPlugin checkpoints</li>
          <li><b>Bet-strategy analytics</b> — your win rate by market type, longest losing streak, favorite team to bet on</li>
          <li><b>Custom team colors / emoji</b></li>
        </ul>
      </div>

      <div class="block">
        <h3>Credits</h3>
        <p>Built by <a href="https://github.com/therealwestninja" target="_blank" rel="noopener">therealwestninja</a> · <a href="https://www.deviantart.com/west-ninja" target="_blank" rel="noopener">@west-ninja</a> on DeviantArt.</p>
        <p>The four-syllable random-name generator is by @WestNinja (Feb 2020), originally adapted from a Shakespearean insult generator by Darren G. Holloway, Jerry Maguire, and J. Kessels.</p>
        <p>Mascot surnames are a custom mix designed for sports-broadcast vibes.</p>
      </div>
    </div>
  </div>
</div>

<!-- ============================================================
     SIMULATION — Part A
     Constants, name generator, position archetypes, league state
     ============================================================ -->
<script>
'use strict';

/* ============================================================
   RNG + MATH HELPERS
   All randomness used by the simulation routes through SIM_RNG, a
   seeded mulberry32 PRNG. Two clients running with the same seed
   produce byte-identical match outcomes — the foundation for shared
   live matches (see ROADMAP.md Tier 1).

   `Math.random()` is preserved for non-simulation purposes (UI
   nicety, visual jitter that doesn't affect outcome). The helpers
   below (R, RI, pick, pickN, shuffle) ALWAYS use the seeded RNG.
   ============================================================ */

// mulberry32 — fast, good distribution, period 2^32
function mulberry32(seed) {
  let s = seed >>> 0;
  return function() {
    s = (s + 0x6D2B79F5) >>> 0;
    let t = s;
    t = Math.imul(t ^ (t >>> 15), t | 1);
    t ^= t + Math.imul(t ^ (t >>> 7), t | 61);
    return ((t ^ (t >>> 14)) >>> 0) / 4294967296;
  };
}

// Hash a string to a uint32 (FNV-1a). Used for stable match seeds.
function hashStr(s) {
  let h = 2166136261 >>> 0;
  for (let i = 0; i < s.length; i++) {
    h ^= s.charCodeAt(i);
    h = Math.imul(h, 16777619);
  }
  return h >>> 0;
}

// Deterministic seed for a given (season, slot) pair. The genesis seed is
// stable per league (set once on initLeague + persisted), so two clients
// loading the same saved state derive the same match seed.
function computeMatchSeed(season, slot) {
  const genesis = (typeof LEAGUE !== "undefined" && LEAGUE.genesisSeed) || 0xF107C001;
  return hashStr(`${genesis}:s${season}:${slot}`);
}

// Global RNG state for the simulation. Seeded at match start (and
// inherited from saved league state for determinism across reloads).
const SIM_RNG = {
  seed: 0,
  next: () => Math.random(), // overwritten on first reseed
  reseed(seed) {
    this.seed = seed >>> 0;
    this.next = mulberry32(this.seed);
  },
};
// Separate RNG for visual-only biome/scatter generation. Reseeded at
// match start with a DIFFERENT transformation of the match seed so its
// stream is independent of the combat RNG. This guarantees that adding
// or removing a biome decoration call in the future never affects
// combat outcomes — visuals and gameplay use independent streams.
const BIOME_RNG = {
  seed: 0,
  next: () => Math.random(),
  reseed(seed) {
    // XOR with a separate constant so this stream diverges from SIM_RNG
    // even when both are seeded from the same match seed.
    this.seed = (seed ^ 0x9E3779B9) >>> 0;
    this.next = mulberry32(this.seed);
  },
};
// Initial seed — replaced as soon as the first match starts. Using a
// fixed integer here means a fresh league always boots identically
// until startMatch() reseeds with a per-match seed.
SIM_RNG.reseed(0xF107C001);
BIOME_RNG.reseed(0xF107C001);

function R(min, max) { return SIM_RNG.next() * (max - min) + min; }
function RI(min, max) { return Math.floor(R(min, max + 1)); }
function pick(arr) { return arr[Math.floor(SIM_RNG.next() * arr.length)]; }
function pickN(arr, n) {
  const c = arr.slice();
  const out = [];
  for (let i = 0; i < n && c.length; i++) {
    out.push(c.splice(Math.floor(SIM_RNG.next() * c.length), 1)[0]);
  }
  return out;
}
function shuffle(arr) {
  const a = arr.slice();
  for (let i = a.length - 1; i > 0; i--) {
    const j = Math.floor(SIM_RNG.next() * (i + 1));
    [a[i], a[j]] = [a[j], a[i]];
  }
  return a;
}
function clamp(v, lo, hi) { return Math.max(lo, Math.min(hi, v)); }
function distance(a, b) {
  const dx = a.x - b.x, dy = a.y - b.y;
  return Math.sqrt(dx*dx + dy*dy);
}

/* ============================================================
   NAME GENERATOR
   Embedded from user's "simple random name generator" (4-syllable
   fantasy-style: Capital + lowercase + middle syllable + ending).
   Produces names like "Qarchlut", "Bearson", "Acackadain".

   To swap in a different generator:
     - Replace the four arrays below, OR
     - Replace genFirstName() to call your source.
   ============================================================ */
const NG_W1 = ["Q","A","B","C","D","E","F","G","H","I","J","K","L","M","N","O","P","R","S","T","U","V","W","X","Y","Z"];
const NG_W2 = ["a","c","e","f","h","l","m","n","o","r","s","t","u","x","y"];
const NG_W3 = ["rch","a","ack","ad","agg","ah","ai","al","an","ana","ar","ara","as","b","c","ch","ck","d","dwyn","e","ea","ear","ee","eira","ek","el","end","er","ex","ey","f","g","gn","h","i","iar","ig","il","ill","im","imma","in","in","inu","io","ion","ip","ir","iri","irm","irsa","is","ish","isil","ito","iz","izu","j","ka","ko","l","la","lar","lbor","le","le","les","ll","lm","ln","ls","m","me","mm","mo","n","na","nd","ne","nk","nt","nt","ntin","o","ock","og","om","on","oo","ory","our","p","ph","r","ra","rc","re","ren","ri","rn","ro","rr","rra","rv","s","se","sh","sh","sl","so","t","ta","te","tz","u","ull","us","us","ve","x","y","ynn","yo","zer"];
const NG_W4 = ["lut","aam","adain","adon","ain","aira","ais","ako","alary","ald","aliam","aline","ama","amari","ami","an","anan","ander","ankem","ano","aot","ar","ari","aroch","aron","ary","as","asha","asia","ay","azan","bbert","brone","cinbe","ck","cko","dai","dain","dalis","ddery","den","dian","dor","eah","eal","eam","ean","edain","ee","een","eif","enain","enar","enard","endon","eous","era","erm","eron","ery","ery","ethel","eve","even","eybol","fer","fir","firem","frey","gal","garom","gawa","gen","ggy","gil","gin","gon","gout","ham","haru","haun","helle","heo","hera","hina","hon","ht","iador","iam","ian","ias","iba","ice","igan","iko","ill","ilus","imor","ine","inus","ion","ira","is","ita","ka","katt","ker","ket","key","ku","la","lain","lash","ld","le","lem","ley","liaf","ll","lliden","loki","ma","main","mar","mara","maros","mer","mi","mie","mon","mor","morn","ndus","ndy","nell","ner","net","nny","nor","ntz","nuary","oconi","odenn","odor","off","old","om","omi","omoru","onton","onus","oole","oot","oot","oram","orm","orn","ory","ouhm","oum","ouos","ous","pher","pike","ra","rah","ran","rd","rea","ree","rem","ren","ri","rio","rlie","rom","ron","ros","rot","rry","run","rynn","shima","shin","son","sose","ta","tac","tain","tall","tar","ter","terian","thain","thor","thorne","tide","to","tone","tor","uce","udain","uib","ull","us","use","use","va","vid","von","wran","wrath","xander","xia","xieam","xoust","yle","zell","zuo"];

function genFirstName() {
  return pick(NG_W1) + pick(NG_W2) + pick(NG_W3) + pick(NG_W4);
}

/* Mascot surnames — give the fantasy first name a sports-flavored last name */
const SURNAMES = [
  "Whiskers","Pawington","Snouter","Furr","Nibbles","Talons","Scales","Thunderclaw",
  "Finsworth","Tailwhip","Brushfoot","Glimmerfang","Hootington","Bubblesnap","Dustfeather",
  "Bristle","Mossback","Quillbeard","Velvetfoot","Pebble","Ironclaw","Dapplecoat",
  "Wartooth","Fizzlepaw","Marshmallow","Pricklesnoot","Chompers","Whisper",
  "Hopstride","Dribblepaw","Snufflenose","Crashfoot"
];
const SUFFIXES = ["", "", "", "", "", "II", "III", "Jr.", "the Bold", "the Ferocious", "the Cute"];

function genFullName() {
  let parts = [genFirstName(), pick(SURNAMES)];
  const sx = pick(SUFFIXES);
  if (sx) parts.push(sx);
  return parts.join(" ");
}

// League-wide config that's user-tunable via the Settings tab
const LEAGUE_CONFIG = {
  bracketSize: 12, // 4 / 8 / 12 (12 = qualifier round → 8-team ladder). v28.13: bumped to 12 for the 20-team continental expansion — 12 of 20 teams play each season, and the qualifier-round format gives top-ranked teams byes for narrative interest
  // Visual / accessibility toggles. All default to false (effects ON).
  // The render layer reads these per-frame so toggling takes immediate effect.
  disableShake: false,
  disableFieldFx: false,   // ambient particles, cloud shadows, slow-mo
  disableLighting: false,  // pitch lights, vignette, player drop shadows
  disableCrowd: false,     // stadium spectators border + cheer-on-KO
  highFrameRate: false,    // false = 30fps cap (default, smooth on most devices)
                           // true  = 60fps cap (matches device refresh, smoother
                           //         but doubles render work). Sim tick rate is
                           //         independent (20fps) so this only changes
                           //         draw frequency, not gameplay speed.
  // === v28 game mode selector + per-mode config ===
  // gameMode determines how a match is structured. "deathmatch" is the
  // canonical 5v5 best-of-N round-elimination mode. The three new modes
  // run as a single continuous "match" with different scoring rules.
  gameMode: "deathmatch",  // "deathmatch" | "koth" | "ctf" | "tag"
  modeConfig: {
    koth: {
      pointsToWin: 50,    // 25 / 50 / 100 supported
      tickPointsPerSec: 1, // points per second for crown holder
    },
    ctf: {
      capsToWin: 5,       // 3 / 5 / 7 / 9
      teamSize: 7,        // bots per team in CTF (request: "teams of 7")
      flagReturnSec: 8,   // seconds before dropped flag returns to center
      carrierSpeedMult: 0.80,
    },
    tag: {
      // "Damage scaled high enough that rounds take under 90 seconds"
      // It stays It, target freezes 3s then unfreezes (per spec). Damage
      // applied: per-hit (small chunk) + bleed during 3s freeze (heavy).
      // Total per-tag = tagDamagePerHit + tagDpsWhileFrozen × 3 sec.
      // With 50 + (130 × 3) = 440 damage, average bots die in 1-2 tags.
      tagFreezeFrames: 60,    // 3 seconds @ 20fps
      tagDamagePerHit: 50,    // immediate damage on tag
      tagDpsWhileFrozen: 130, // bleed dps during 3-second freeze
    },
  },
};

/* ============================================================
   POSITION ARCHETYPES — 5 distinct roles, one per team slot
   Each archetype has stat-tendency offsets applied AFTER random
   base rolls, so individual variance is preserved.

   Stats: HP (cap 150), DMG (cap 40), DEF (cap 50), SPD (cap 4)
   Crit chance is 8% baseline; sniper raises it.
   ============================================================ */
const POSITIONS = [
  // === Position bias (v28 rebalance) ===
  // Tank: classic damage-sponge — much higher HP, much higher defense,
  // much lower speed, no crit chance bonus.
  { id: "tank",     icon: "🛡️", label: "Tank",       cls: "pos-tank",
    bias: { hp: +60, dmg: -8,  def: +18, spd: -0.8, crit: 0     } },
  // Striker: balanced offense — solid damage, modest crit.
  { id: "striker",  icon: "⚔️", label: "Striker",    cls: "pos-striker",
    bias: { hp:  +5, dmg: +10, def:  +2, spd:  0,   crit: +0.04 } },
  // Speed: hit-and-run — very fast, fragile, hits OK with crit upside.

    bias: { hp: -15, dmg:  +5, def:  -6, spd: +1.5, crit: +0.05 } },
